#2887. Credit card debt climbs to a record $1.29 trillion, New York Fed research finds

Americans are increasingly turning to credit cards as financial pressures mount, new reports show.

Collectively, credit card balances climbed by $22 billion in the third quarter to a record $1.29 trillion, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's latest household debt report, released Tuesday. Balances are up 4.2% from a year earlier.

Total household debt, including mortgages, auto loans and student loans, also expanded by $334 billion for the July-to-September period to a record $19.1 trillion.

"Overall debt has been growing," the New York Fed researchers said on a press call Tuesday. "This does reflect financial hardship out there."

The average credit card balance per consumer now stands at $6,678, up 2.4% year over year, a separate quarterly credit industry insights report from TransUnion found. The number of people with credit cards rose 2% to 178 million from 174.8 million.

Consumers often turn to credit cards in part because they are more accessible than other types of loans — even though the average interest rate on a new credit card offer is about 24%, according to data from LendingTree. Credit card APRs vary depending on the type of card, the issuer and the borrower's creditworthiness.

"Credit cards continue to be one of the most expensive ways to borrow," said Charlie Wise, TransUnion's senior vice president of global research and consulting.

Over time, an increasing share of new credit cards has gone to borrowers with lower incomes and credit scores, and "those accounts are now showing higher levels of distress," the New York Fed researchers said in a corresponding blog post.

Credit card issuers loosened their lending standards during the Covid pandemic, and delinquency rates rose in the years that followed, the New York Fed researchers said on the press call.

"Macroeconomic headwinds — namely high inflation and rising borrowing costs — have squeezed consumers, with inflation outpacing wage growth for low-income brackets," according to the New York Fed.

Meanwhile, the cost of living continues to climb, with Americans paying more for essentials such as groceries and gas.

Stubborn inflation has now stretched on for years, putting many households in an affordability squeeze. Costs for goods and services rose more than expected again in October, the Bureau of Labor Statistics also reported Tuesday.

"Higher prices and higher rates have pushed more people into debt," said Ted Rossman, principal consumer finance analyst at Money Management International, a nonprofit credit counseling agency.

Balances have continued to swell in the wake of the pandemic, and even though the growth rate is now slowing, "from a household finance standpoint, a lot of damage has been done," Rossman said.

The reports come on the heels of the Democrats' strong midterm results, when many voters expressed their dissatisfaction with the U.S. economy, according to Kyle Moore, chief economist at the nonpartisan think tank The Century Foundation.

"The throughline connecting so many of Tuesday's outcomes was a recognition that the American economy is failing American families," Moore said, referring to Election Day.

#2886. Jaylen Brown takes his medicine after rough night against his old team

Those lamb chops did not come out as well as hoped.

In the most highly anticipated game of this young regular season, Jaylen Brown finally took on Jayson Tatum and the Boston Celtics after thinly veiled barbs had been tossed by both sides since the summer. It did not go Brown or the Sixers' way.

Brown played just 25 minutes thanks to a combination of foul trouble and a waving of the white flag, finishing with 13 points on 4-of-9 shooting along with five rebounds, two assists and four turnovers. The Sixers trailed the majority of the night and lost 123-101. After Philly tied the game at 76 late in the third quarter, the Celtics outscored them 47-25 over the final 16 minutes of the game.

To pour salt in the wound, Paul George, the player the Sixers sent to Boston in return for Brown, dropped a game-high 36 points. After the game Brown was asked if he was relieved that this game is behind him now.

"I guess you could say that," he said. "I'm a bit frustrated, honestly. I wanted to perform better for my team. Foul trouble kind of derailed me a little bit, but it happens. It's unfortunate, but now we got to just move on to the next [game]."

Brown was ready to take his medicine. He said multiple times that the Celtics outplayed and out-executed the Sixers. On top of the foul trouble, he thought the Celtics did a good job game planning for him, pointing out that he got double teamed on every drive. His coach felt similarly.

"It started really tough for him in the beginning by getting in foul trouble right from the start, and then he gets another early one in the second half, so he really had no chance to get going and [it] made it tough," said Nick Nurse. "I think they did a pretty good job of swarming him and sending multiple defenders. I really think that, especially in the first half, we were generating really good shots out of their swarming defense."

The Sixers once again struggled to make open jumpers, finishing the night making 29% of their 37 three-point attempts. Even as the team's performance turned the nationally televised game into a dud, there were still plenty of eyeballs on Tatum and Brown. Would they interact and if so, how much?

The answer was not very much. Right before tip-off Tatum went over to dap-up Brown as well as a couple other Sixers and Brown completely no-sold it.

"I'm just focused on coming out and doing what's best for my team. Everything else was just, to be honest, I wasn't paying attention," Brown said of the interaction. "Credit to Jayson, he played well tonight. Credit to the Celtics, they executed, they shot the hell out of the ball, and they were the better team tonight."

Brown also insisted that there weren't any extra emotions for him coming into this game. He said he was locked in and focused, and had his rhythm derailed by picking up those two early fouls. At the same time, he admitted that this was a game he wanted to play well in.

"I was looking forward to this, I was looking forward to playing well," he said, "but shit happens, you just get ready for the next one I guess."

While this game was the most anticipated around the league so far, it won't be the last game that has, for lack of a better term, external noise. It was even the second game of that nature the Sixers had played in their last five days, with LeBron James making a return to Cleveland just a few days prior.

Tyrese Maxey explained how the team deals with those games as a group. It's important for the group to do so because he doesn't think those types of games are going to stop coming for this team.

"I think the biggest thing was just trying to be there for him. It is going to be emotional no matter how the game," Maxey said. "I think he did a good job of just, hanging in there. I know it was tough, he got into foul trouble early, and it's a difficult situation, but it's OK."

The good news for Brown is that Maxey is right, these games won't stop coming this season. He'll have at least three more chances to get his revenge against Boston and after the Sixers performance in this one, the noise will only be getting louder.

#2884. Hegseth impeachment effort torpedoed in House

The House of Representatives effectively torpedoed an effort to impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth with a procedural vote on Tuesday evening.

The House, by a 211-204 margin, approved a measure that will refer the impeachment resolution to a committee.

That will leave little, if any, time for the committee and full House to act on it before Democrats take over the chamber and the Senate with a new majority in early January.

House Republican leadership had sought to kill the effort at impeachment, which was initiated Monday by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky.

The vote came a week after the midterm elections, where Democrats trounced Republicans in the first congressional election since the Iran war broke out in February.

Hegseth had faced eight articles of impeachment.

Massie accused Hegseth of waging war in contravention of Congress, carrying out extrajudicial killings tied to boat strikes in the Caribbean against purported drug smugglers and suppressing free speech by targeting Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., a military veteran.

Massie is a libertarian who lost his Republican primary in May after clashing with President Donald Trump.

The Kentucky Republican led a bill last year to compel the release of files related to convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Shortly after the vote, Trump said: "Democrats' attempt to impeach Secretary of War Pete Hegseth has miserably FAILED!"

"Every single Republican, except Thomas Massie, a major LOSER, voted in favor of Pete," the president said, declaring what he said was an "EXONERATION" of Hegseth.

A Republican forced the vote on Hegseth's impeachment. All Democrats voted against referring the measure to committee.

Defense Department spokesperson Jacob Bliss said earlier in the day that Hegseth "carried out direct, lawful orders from the President to secure the nation." The impeachment effort notably focused on Hegseth, not Trump, who is responsible for launching the various military operations referred to in the articles.

Hegseth, at a press conference at the Pentagon earlier Tuesday, railed against Massie and defended his leadership in the Iran war and other military operations.

"What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it's not accountability. It's a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment," Hegseth said

"It's a farce, and it's a stunt, and everybody knows it," Hegseth said. "It puts a target on the backs of American warriors by signaling that Congress will undermine their missions in real time."This is not patriotism. This is sabotage."

Trump, in a Truth Social post on Tuesday, said Hegseth "gets an 'A' PLUS, PLUS, PLUS for his defense against a disgruntled, angry, defeated, former Congressman, Thomas Massie, a TOTAL LOSER!"

Trump said Monday that he would stand by Hegseth even if the House impeached him. The president was impeached by the House two times during his first White House term. The Senate acquitted him in both cases.

Republicans hold a slim 218-214 majority in the House.

Some Republicans, notably including Vice President JD Vance, have directly blamed their midterm losses on the Iran war, which continues to see intermittent fighting amid an uneasy stalemate.

Neither of the two Cabinet secretaries impeached by the House has ever been removed by the Senate after a trial in that chamber.

#2881. Hundreds of schools in Italy, UK evacuated over 'hoax' bomb threats

Hundreds of schools across Europe were evacuated on Wednesday after receiving bomb threats, police forces say, in what appeared to be the latest wave of efforts to intimidate populations.

Italy's education minister Giuseppe Valditara told Italian TV that the emailed threats were presumed to be hoaxes, and that the number of schools affected was expected to rise.

Threats, received just before 10 a.m. local time by more than 300 schools, were signed "Mikhail Andropov, leader of the Vilayat of Misoginistan," according to a copy of the message seen by Reuters. The same purported sender also claimed responsibility for bomb threats emailed to newsrooms in Ireland and Bulgaria on Friday, according to Reuters.

Schools in Scotland and Northern Ireland were also evacuated on Wednesday morning after receiving similar threats.

The Police Service of Northern Ireland said they were treated as a hoax, while Police Scotland similarly said there was nothing to suggest the threats posed any credible risk to the public.

"Based on enquiries and information to date, police are treating this email, which originated from outside the UK, as a hoax," Police Scotland said in a statement.

It comes as concerns grow in Europe about so-called hybrid threats and the continent's ability to withstand such threats.

Hybrid warfare combines tactics such as cyberattacks, sabotage and disinformation to destabilize adversaries without triggering open military conflict.

A flurry of events disrupted supermarkets, blood banks and passenger flights last week.

Bomb threats repeatedly affected flights across Europe in what officials and airlines described as "hoax bomb threats" or "false attacks."

It remains unclear who might be behind the latest hybrid attacks, and whether they could be state-backed.

France on Tuesday said a wave of cyberattacks targeting supermarket fridges was carried out by Shahid Kaveh, a cyber unit it said was affiliated with the Iranian Revolutionary Guard. Iran denied involvement, calling accusations "baseless and malicious."

Incidents come against a backdrop of heightened geopolitical tensions and domestic unrest across parts of Europe, and experts told CNBC that civilian infrastructure could become an increasingly attractive target for hostile actors.

#2879. Investors pivot to defensive stocks as markets enter a ‘state of alert’

Markets are in a "state of alert," according to analysts monitoring a pivot from riskier tech sector bets into defensive stocks.

Technology stocks — a key driver of rallies in recent years — have been notably volatile in 2026. Concerns about artificial intelligence — particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing — have sparked multiple bouts of volatility this year.

The Democrats' sweep of Congress in the U.S. midterm elections amplified pressure on the sector last week.

Wall Street's tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index is up by around 8.7% since the start of the year as of Nov. 9 close, but gave up some of its year-to-date gains in October when it shed 5.3% over the course of the month. The index — home to Big Tech names including Nvidia, Microsoft, Alphabet and Apple — has staged a recovery in recent days, but remains in the red for November and is down 0.7% for the month so far after dipping into correction territory last week.

The Nasdaq isn't the only tech-focused index to have seen big losses over the past month. South Korea's Kospi index, which is heavily exposed to the semiconductor sector, lost 16.2% in October and is down more than 8% month-to-date.

The MSCI World Information Technology index lost 7% in October, and has shed a further 4.6% so far this month. MSCI's U.S.-only IT index is down 11.5% since October.

At the same time, stocks in defensive sectors have seen broad gains.

MSCI's indexes encompassing the consumer staples, healthcare and utilities sectors — widely seen as more stable assets in the equity market during times of volatility or uncertainty — have all risen significantly over the last month.

In a note on Monday, Alastair Pinder, HSBC's head emerging markets and global equity strategist, said recent moves in stock markets had been "vicious, and in our view, overdone."

"The market is already pricing greater execution risk and capex delays," he said, referring to the development of artificial intelligence. "With US semiconductors trading on a 12-month forward PE of 13.5x after a 45% de-rating since June, how much more pain is there to price?"

He added that fundamentals broadly appear healthy.

"The rotation into defensives looks extreme," he said. "Walmart trades on 40x forward earnings, near a record high, while Nvidia trades on 13x, near a record low."

Russ Mould, investment director at AJ Bell, described equity markets as being "in a state of alert, judging by how investors are rotating their sector exposures."

"Such movement matters, and can help stock pickers with their selections, bearing in mind the old adage that 'a bad stock in a good sector will outperform a good stock in a bad sector,'" he said in a note on Monday.

AJ Bell's analysis of 11 so-called "super-sectors" in the S&P Global 1200 shows that, in the second half of 2026 so far, healthcare and consumer staples stocks have been the best performers after energy — a major beneficiary of surging oil prices in the wake of the U.S.-Iran war.

The IT sector, the data shows, has gone from being the best-performing sector in the first half of the year on a 28.4% rise, to the worst performer in the second half after tumbling more than 11%.

"Information Technology has gone from penthouse to outhouse and slumped from being the best performer to the worst," Mould said.

"Energy has been resilient as have Consumer Staples and Healthcare, while Industrials have been noticeably weak. These trends could reflect nagging worries over the economic outlook for next year, as well as worries over whether air is leaking out of an AI 'bubble.'"

#2878. 'Malcolm in the Middle' star, race car driver Frankie Muniz hospitalized after Daytona crash

DAYTONA BEACH, Fla. (AP) — Actor and race car driver Frankie Muniz was hospitalized Wednesday following a practice-lap crash at Daytona International Speedway, according to his racing team.

Team Reaume said the "Malcolm in the Middle" star was "awake and alert."

In 2024, Muniz ran 38 races each on the NASCAR O'Reilly Auto Parts and trucks series.

He's participated in 117 trucks series races over the last three years, with one top-10 finish at Daytona in 2025.

#2877. White House vows to press ahead with tariffs despite midterm backlash over rising prices

One week after voters punished Republicans over rising prices in the midterm elections, President Donald Trump's White House signaled it would stay the course on tariffs, even as his former top economic advisor warned that Americans are footing the bill.

Jim Goyer, Trump's director of White House Office of Public Liaison, defended tariffs as a tool to rebuild U.S. manufacturing, secure supply chains and protect national security during an address Tuesday at the B20 summit, a gathering of global business leaders in Washington hosted by the U.S. Chamber of Commerce.

"And we are just getting started," Goyer said. "Our ambition is to continue this pace for the remainder of the president's term in office."

But Gary Cohn, who served as Trump's chief economic advisor during his first administration, offered a sharply different assessment during an onstage interview later Tuesday.

"Tariffs are a consumption tax on the U.S. consumer," Cohn said, arguing that the duties are raising prices and forcing Americans to reconsider purchases.

The divide exposed a growing dilemma for Trump. His trade agenda is reshaping global commerce – and driving up costs for voters. Companies are adapting to the chaos, but the old trading system isn't coming back.

"President Trump has disrupted that system in a way that we're not likely to return to," Kevin Brady, a former Republican congressman who helped lead the B20 summit, said during a discussion with U.S. Chamber CEO Suzanne Clark.

The political backlash was evident in the Nov. 3 election. Roughly two-thirds of voters said Trump's policies contributed to inflation, according to the CNBC Voter Poll. Nearly nine in 10 said the cost of living was a major or crucial factor in their vote.

A separate Reuters/Ipsos poll released Tuesday found 62% of Americans supported repealing Trump's tariffs, while just 21% approved of his handling of the cost of living.

"Last week, American voters sent a clear message about the cost of living and their economic security," Clark said in her keynote address.

The pressure intensified Tuesday as new government data showed consumer prices rose 0.4% in October, exceeding economists' expectations. Annual inflation reached 3.7% in September, the latest available year-over-year figure and well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target.

Tariffs are not the only factor driving higher prices. The war with Iran has pushed energy costs sharply higher, while housing, healthcare and other household expenses continue to strain consumers.

But research has found Trump's import duties have added to those pressures. A Federal Reserve Bank of New York study estimated that about 26% of tariff increases were passed through to consumer prices, with some effects taking nine to 12 months to work through supply chains.

Cohn, now vice chairman of IBM, argued that while tariffs generate revenue for the U.S., those gains come at consumers' expense.

"You're looking at one side of the equation. You're looking at the credit side of the balance sheet," Cohn said, describing how he would respond to Trump's defense of tariff revenue. "There's the debit side of the balance sheet, which is every hardworking American who happened to pay the tariff through some product that they bought."

Still, Cohn saw room for a bipartisan trade deal. He suggested Trump and the incoming Democratic Congress could make protections against China and unfair trade practices permanent while rolling back tariffs on Canada, leaving less for future presidents to undo.

For businesses, the shift is already underway. UPS CEO Carol Tomé said customers are moving production and sourcing closer to home as global supply chains become more regional.

"Supply chains are diversifying. Supply chains are regionalizing," Tomé said during a panel discussion moderated by CNBC's Morgan Brennan.

Separately, UPS is opening a new $80 million-plus air hub in the Philippines in October to expand its Asian network and improve connections across more than 1,100 global trade lanes.

Other executives, like Jim Fitterling, executive chairman of chemicals giant Dow, defended Trump's tariffs despite their costs. Fitterling acknowledged they can drive up prices but argued they are necessary to combat unfair competition and protect U.S. manufacturing.

Democrats plan to challenge Trump's tariffs when they take control of Congress in January, but his veto power and broad trade authority could make them difficult to overturn.

#2875. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2874. Dodgers, Mets, Padres, Blue Jays emerge as Tarik Skubal suitors as market for top MLB free agent takes shape

LAS VEGAS — As the MLB general managers meetings got underway Tuesday, speculation started to ramp up regarding the offseason's top free agent, Tarik Skubal. This year, the 29-year-old superstar left-hander is in a tier of his own.

Skubal is not only this offseason's No. 1 free agent, but he's also the best starting pitcher available by far. While Freddy Peralta, Michael King, Trevor Rogers, Clay Holmes and others are strong rotation options for contenders, none of them is the clear-cut ace that Skubal is.

"It's very clear that it's a dominant and strong Sku-bull market," agent Scott Boras said Wednesday in Las Vegas, offering one of his signature free-agency puns.

"In the deep waters of talent treasure, there's no time to come up for air. Teams have filled their tanks, and they're all clearly ready for the Skubal dive," he continued.

As usual, several MLB teams are looking for starting pitching on this year's free-agent market, but because of the price the two-time Cy Young Award winner is expected to command, two teams have emerged as the early front-runners for Skubal's services: the Los Angeles Dodgers and the New York Mets.

The Dodgers, who acquired Skubal from Detroit ahead of the August trade deadline, have a robust assortment of starting pitching options already in-house, including Blake Snell, Tyler Glasnow, Justin Wrobleski, Roki Sasaki and Shohei Ohtani. Even so, it's clear the free-agent ace made an impact while in Dodger blue.

"He was awesome getting to know [this season]. Fit in great in the clubhouse," Dodgers general manager Brandon Gomes said. "Obviously, we know what kind of pitcher he is. For us, it's understanding what this all looks like, what does our team need, what does the contract look like. We know we love the player. We love the human. Now we kind of have to play out to see what all that looks like."

Despite the Dodgers' stated interest in bringing him back, the Mets are viewed as the favorites to land Skubal. New York lacks a clear ace to pair with right-hander Nolan McLean at the top of their rotation, and after things did not go as hoped for Peralta in Queens in 2026, Skubal represents a chance at redemption for the Mets this offseason.

New York has also shown a willingness to pay at the top of the market under owner Steve Cohen, most notably with the team's decision to make Juan Soto the highest paid player in the history of professional sports. Still, since the arrival of president of baseball operations David Stearns, the Mets haven't come to terms on a long-term deal with a starting pitcher.

"Every situation is unique. It's been well-publicized, even since I've been here, we've engaged in extremely long-term negotiations for [starting] pitchers," Stearns said. "We will do so again, but [the] situation matters, the specific circumstance matters. We do have to look at every individual pitcher on a case-by-case basis."

Stearns went on to discuss the Mets' status as an appealing free-agent destination.

"I think it starts with our ownership and organizational commitment to winning," he said. "And since Steve and Alex Cohen have bought the team, they've spared no expense to do everything they possibly can to to win, to demonstrate to players, to demonstrate to players' families, that this is a desirable location, and when players come here and they see that, I think it becomes a very attractive place to play."

While the Dodgers and Mets will dominate much of the speculation around the two-time Cy Young Award winner, one of the biggest questions over the next several weeks is which teams are actually in position to acquire Skubal, given his price point?

It has been reported that Skubal and Boras are planning to meet with the San Diego Padres and the Toronto Blue Jays in the coming weeks, but the sheer size of Skubal's desired contract will limit the number of legitimate suitors.

Over the past few years, it has come to be expected that Skubal will look to top the average annual value record of $43.3 million set by Max Scherzer and Justin Verlander as well as the total pitcher contract value record of $325 million set by Yoshinobu Yamamoto in 2023.

"The rarity of the [Greg] Madduxes, the [Randy] Johnsons, the [Hunter] Browns, the [Gerrit] Coles. When you go back and look at the [Max] Scherzers, all these true No. 1 [Barry] Zitos, they've all led to world championships," Boras said. "That's kind of what the teams are about. … If we live over a 30- or 40-year period, we might have less than 10 of these aces that are capable of winning multiple Cy Youngs. And what that means is having … an opportunity to win a championship."

Even with the collective bargaining agreement set to expire in less than three weeks, Boras doesn't sound like he feels any pressure to hurry to get a deal done for Skubal. Five years ago, the super-agent agreed to contracts worth a combined $500 million for free-agent clients Corey Seager and Marcus Semien right before the 2021 CBA expiration.

With baseball's economics a much bigger talking point this time around, we'll see if he can get a mega-deal done before the deadline once again.

#2873. Jayson Tatum, Jaylen Brown, and the myth of NBA friendships

PHILADELPHIA – The last time Jayson Tatum and Jaylen Brown shared the floor was in late April, in Game 6 of the first round of the NBA playoffs.

Neither knew it then, but that lackluster game, a Philadelphia 76ers blowout, became their last as teammates. Tatum missed the subsequent Game 7 with a leg injury, and Brown was stunningly traded just a few months later to the very Northeast rival that ended their nine-year run as teammates.

Four months later, Brown turned a cold shoulder to Tatum moments before tip-off of Tuesday's highly-anticipated game between the 76ers and the Celtics.

With cameras and reporters flooding Xfinity Mobile Arena, the two stars never spoke or embraced, at least not publicly. Tatum punted Brown-related questions postgame, and Brown denied any ill intent behind the viral pregame exchange.

"To be honest, I wasn't paying attention," Brown said.

"No headlines about anything like that," Tatum said when pressed on the interaction. "I was just getting ready for the game, ready for the jump ball, go out there and compete, have some fun, and try to get a win."

Still, despite Tatum's best efforts to downplay the storyline, the coverage leading up to Tuesday's game between the 76ers and the Celtics centered on a now-fractured, highly publicized relationship between two superstars, one that has been analyzed and debated for nearly a decade. So many media members attended the game that Tatum said it felt like an NBA Finals game — and joked he didn't expect to see most of us in Toronto the next day.

As someone who has covered dozens of Celtics playoff games, I can say the atmosphere felt heightened. The Celtics bench rose to its feet after nearly every play. Payton Pritchard and Paul George let out screams after back-to-back 3-pointers. Tatum jumped up emphatically and clenched his fist when Neemias Queta secured a putback over Joel Embiid.

The game pitted the East's top two teams against each other, but more than anything, it was billed as a showdown between two former teammates turned foes. Brown struggled: he finished with a game-worst -27, four turnovers, four fouls, and a season-low 13 points. Tatum had the much better night, with 21 points, six rebounds, four assists, and two blocks, including a throwdown dunk on LeBron James that sent Hugo Gonzalez and the Celtics bench into a frenzy.

But ultimately, despite all the hype, neither player fittingly ended the night as the top performer on their team.

That morning, at Celtics shootaround, "Why Can't We Be Friends?" blasted as players warmed up. Perhaps it was a coincidence, but the song was a fitting backdrop for the night and commotion ahead.

Much of the reason the Tatum-Brown storyline has captivated the basketball world is that fans are so eager for players on their team to be friends.

Were they ever actually friends? Does it even matter?

For much of the past decade, the questions followed Tatum and Brown everywhere they went:

Do they actually like each other? Do they hang out? Do they secretly want the other one gone?

Every pregame dap-up and high-five seemed to prove they got along. Their (relatively) limited on-court synergy suggested otherwise. Every postgame answer about the other, and there were many, was scrutinized. Some left more room for interpretation than others, but hardly any felt completely satisfactory, at least not to the heaviest Brown-Tatum skeptics.

In 2024, when Brown won Eastern Conference Finals MVP and Finals MVP, all eyes were on Tatum's reaction.

Was Tatum happy for Brown? Was he envious?

In the 2023 offseason, the duo made headlines with a joint, on-camera workout in Los Angeles, a rarity in their tenure. For those in the 'Brown and Tatum are friends' contingent, the moment felt like a victory lap. That contingent reached the mountaintop when the Celtics won the 2024 championship, and Brown earnestly credited Tatum in his Finals MVP speech.

"I share this with my brothers and my partner in crime, Jayson Tatum," Brown said as confetti fell. "He was with me the whole way. So, we share this shit together."

But even at that peak, Brown and Tatum were not particularly close friends, according to multiple sources. They were certainly in a better place than they were on Tuesday night; there's no question about that.

But the notion that the two All-Stars were great friends was misguided.

So, for those who viewed the ice-cold exchange before tip-off as the punctuation mark on a public breakup, as the final act for body-language doctors and psychoanalysts to dissect — the reality is more complicated, more nuanced, and far less exciting, at least in my view.

NBA friendships differ sharply from the teammate friendships most of us know from middle and high school sports and other non-professional settings. NBA players have egos, money, and fame that are hard for the average person to relate to. They are surrounded by their own 'camps' – the people in their inner circle who look out for them and their careers, whether that's family members, friends, personal trainers, podcast co-hosts, photographers, agents, or publicists.

Those are the people players, especially the highest-profile ones, often get closest to: people whose job, by definition, is to look out for their best interests. On Tuesday night, key members of both players' camps were in Philadelphia, watching the two face off from the sidelines.

The incessant media coverage and fanfare are rooted in an assumption rarely challenged in sports coverage and discourse: that the guys on your team should be friends, that the best players should be particularly close, and that a championship comes as the end result when a group of talented people who love each other plays basketball together.

But the people who actually manage NBA locker rooms will tell you that's not how it works, at least not the vast majority of the time.

"I'll tell you right now, all 30 teams, it's not Boy Scouts. We're not all just sitting there getting along, and everyone's happy-go-lucky," Joe Mazzulla said before tip-off. "I mean, that's just not how the world works… We're going after something that's highly competitive and stressful, and there's things at stake, and with that comes conflict. Doesn't mean it's a bad thing; you work through things together."

Mazzulla noted how much differing lifestyles play a role, too.

"You got people that are single, there are married, divorced, kids, no kids," he said. "People have been traded four times; people haven't been traded at all. You just have a plethora of experiences from on and off the court that you have to mix together into a group of guys, and you have to leave space for that."

So, it's important to note that Mazzulla and other NBA coaches are not striving to build a team of best friends, though those friendships do naturally blossom on occasion. Instead, he's aiming to build a team that understands each other well enough to chase the same thing and set its issues aside for a common goal, a locker room filled with guys who respect one another, even if they don't always see eye to eye.

"I think the one thing I'd like people to understand is this isn't supposed to be summer camp where we all just get along all the time," Mazzulla said. "There's plenty of times within a great culture, good culture, where you have disagreements… That's just a part of the competitive journey and going after greatness together."

"How can we get to the point where we just go after greatness together, but at the same time appreciate each other's life?"

76ers coach Nick Nurse put it more bluntly.

"The players usually understand, first and foremost, it's a business, right?" Nurse said. "And almost no matter where they're at in these relationships, these things can happen where they get separated because of the business aspect of it."

Nurse suggested the friendships that fans picture belong to an earlier stage of sport.

"Obviously, you kind of hope sports bring a lifelong friendship in those kind of situations," Nurse said. "I'm not sure that's the case in professional nearly as much."

NBA players describe the same gap from the inside; different family structures and lifestyles make genuine, long-term friendships rare.

"I think it's just the stages of life," former Celtics-turned-76ers center Tacko Fall told CelticsBlog. "When you are in high school, for example, you're young, right? You don't have a family to go back to and everything like that. As you get older, you become a professional. You have guys that have wives. You have guys that have kids."

Payton Pritchard, who was teammates with Tatum and Brown for six seasons, added that managing egos plays a role, too.

"There's a lot of egos in it, and people need an ego to get to the NBA. And so it's definitely a balancing act," Pritchard said. "Not to say that we're not all buddies, but everybody's got families. They're trying to make it. They got things to prove, so it's different."

None of this means NBA friendships don't exist – they certainly do.

Brown and Fall, now teammates in Philadelphia, have been very close since their days together in Boston, for example. Brown is the godfather of Fall's youngest daughter, and the two often travel together in the offseason.

"Jaylen's basically become family," Fall said. "We started hanging out so much, and we're just very similar in the way we are as people, in the way we think as well. And then, obviously, the more you hang out with someone, you kind of build those connections. Then our families kind of start to know each other even more, and that relationship just gets better and better."

In Boston, it's well-documented that Hugo Gonzalez and Baylor Scheierman have become fast friends.

In New York, it's well-known that Jalen Brunson, Josh Hart, and Mikal Bridges are close friends, though that friendship originated when they were teammates in college.

But those genuinely close off-court bonds are more of the exception than the norm. In my experience covering the team, players don't hang out off the court nearly as much as fans think. The goal isn't friendship; it's professionalism, respect, and a willingness to set differences aside to win. It's role acceptance. It's discipline.

For the majority of their tenure, Tatum and Brown did that as well as any duo in this league.

"It's working relationships," Pritchard said. "Some people are closer than others, but find a way to win and keep going."

Paul George, fresh off a 36-point outing on Tuesday, said the most fun part of the Celtics season so far wasn't going off against the team that traded him — it's been the team bonding off the court.

"Sorry to give you the most cliché answer ever," he said, "but the most fun is just the camaraderie, being around these guys, the locker room, the flights. That's the most fun that I've had in a while. It's just, again, a good group of guys out there competing together, and then it transfers on the court. You see how much fun we have off the court by how we play on the court."

Pritchard echoed that sentiment, and credited Celtics president of basketball operations Brad Stevens for building a roster of guys who are easy to get along with.

"I just think it starts from the top, and got to give credit to Brad," he said. "He picks really good people to be a part of it, and it's not just about basketball. It's the type of person they are. They're hardworking. They're good locker room guys and stuff like that. So we have a great group, and everybody enjoys being around each other. So that makes a difference. You want to see the next man win. We want to see PG kill. We want to see JT go off. It really matters. So everybody's gonna have their nights, and we love to see it."

And, Tatum's enjoying the good vibes, too.

"We've really kind of bonded really, really well off the court, and I think it's translated on the court," he said. "We got a great group of guys, a bunch of funny individuals. It's been cool."

Maybe one day, Tatum and Brown will sit side-by-side on a podcast, years removed from their playing days, and talk about what went awry in their final act. Maybe one day, they'll return to TD Garden for a marquee game as fans, just as members of the 2008 Celtics team have so many times.

Though their tenure as teammates has ended at long last, it feels inevitable that all eyes will be on their relationship moving forward, regardless.

The scrutiny and questions will likely follow them forever.

But for now, it should be understood that what Brown and Tatum did for 9 seasons was co-work as well as almost any duo in modern basketball.

They played in a whopping 107 playoff games as teammates. They reached five Eastern Conference Finals and two NBA Finals.

Most importantly, they won it all, bringing the Larry O'Brien back to Boston for the first time in 16 years.

By the time Tuesday's game ended, Tatum had already shifted his focus to Wednesday's game against the Toronto Raptors.

"They all count as one," Tatum said, as he has so many times in his years.

That was Tatum's message to his team, too.

"Tonight in the locker room, he said it means nothing if we don't play the way we need to play tomorrow," Mazzulla said. "That's just the nature of the business."

And Jayson Tatum — and Jaylen Brown, for that matter — have both been very good at that business.

#2871. Paul George gives 'a**hole' Sixers fan the finger late in Celtics' blowout win

It didn't get nearly as much attention as the fact that Jaylen Brown would be squaring off against the Boston Celtics for the first time in his career, but Tuesday night's matchup with the Philadelphia 76ers also marked Paul George's first trip back to Philly since being dealt to Boston for Brown in the summertime blockbuster that launched a thousand references to beef, lamb chops, gravy and all manner of other comestibles.

It's understandable that George's return didn't resonate as much as the face-off between Brown and Jayson Tatum, given the nine years they spent together in Boston and the unpleasant public falling-out that has come since the trade. George, after all, wasn't a lifer in Philly to nearly the extent that Brown was in Boston.

"Had I been here eight, nine, 10 years, it'd be a different story," George said, according to Adam Himmelsbach of the Boston Globe. "I was here two years. [...] Obviously, it's a tremendous fan base. A great sports town to play in. So that was appreciated. There's no ill will for me. It'll be just fun to compete. Outside of that, it's a normal game."

Sometimes, though, even a normal game can get a little testy.

After drilling his fourth 3-pointer of the night to give the visitors an 18-point lead with just over 9:30 remaining in the fourth quarter, cameras caught George giving something else to one lucky attendee at Xfinity Mobile Arena:

So, Paul: What happened to turn "no ill will for me" into "flipping some guy the bird?"

"There was a fan being an a**hole," George said in the locker room after the game. "[The] ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if, like, I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do. And, so … yeah. So then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

The pointed salute was far from the only portion of screw-you that the six-time All-NBA selection served up on Tuesday:

George ripped off a 14-5 run by himself over a four-minute span in the fourth quarter, turning Tuesday's tilt into a laugher. He'd finish with a game-high 36 points on 14-for-22 shooting, including a 6-for-11 mark from 3-point range, with five rebounds and an assist in 30 minutes of work as the Celtics knocked off the rival Sixers, 123-101, in a matchup of the top two seeds in the Eastern Conference.

"He took ownership of those fourth-quarter minutes. That's what we needed," Celtics coach Joe Mazzulla said after the game, according to Tara Sullivan of the Boston Globe. "You can't run away from the obvious things that are going on, but he's been traded [three] times, so not the first time he had to go through something like that."

The lights-out performance continued a stellar start to the season for George, who's averaging 22 points, 5.2 rebounds, 3.8 assists, 2.1 steals and 4.9 deflections per game for the Celtics, shooting 49% from the field, 44% from 3-point range and 82% at the free-throw line while playing excellent defense.

George made it clear after the trade that he viewed his role in his new surroundings as complementary — providing support for incumbent superstar Tatum, and doing whatever was necessary to help him elevate the Celtics back into championship contention. He did exactly that on Tuesday, turning what was pitched as a highly emotional contest for Tatum (who finished with 21 points, 6 rebounds and 4 assists) into an evening where the outcome ceased to be in doubt midway through the final frame.

"At this point in my career, there's only one thing I want to do, and that's win," George said during a postgame interview with NBC. "For him, especially, for JT, is to alleviate any pressure, any friction. I'm here to help you. I'm here to serve you, make you become the best player that you can become."

By playing like he has to start the season, helping the Celtics to a 9-2 record with top-five units on both offense and defense, George isn't just alleviating pressure on Tatum. He's also applying plenty of it to the opposition, ensuring that Boston hasn't skipped a beat since that massive summertime trade … and leaving opposing fans muttering some of the same curses under their breath that George preferred to communicate digitally on Tuesday.

#2870. Rep. Jim Jordan, Sen. John Thune elected GOP leaders in Congress

Republican lawmakers on Tuesday picked Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio and Sen. John Thune of South Dakota to serve as their respective House and Senate minority leaders in the next Congress.

Jordan, the House Judiciary Committee chairman, was elected in a 106-73 vote, sources told MS NOW on condition of anonymity to discuss the private meeting. He beat Rep. Steve Scalise, R-La., the current House majority leader.

A two-decade House veteran and committed supporter of President Donald Trump, Jordan will take on the leadership role just as Republicans relinquish power, following blowout Democratic victories in last week's midterm election.

House Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., announced the day after Election Day that he would not run for minority leader in the 120th Congress.

Thune, in a statement, said he "appreciated the opportunity to have an open, honest, and transparent conversation with my colleagues today, and I am honored to have their continued trust and support to lead the Senate Republican Conference in the 120th Congress."

"I'm proud of what this Republican team has accomplished together, and we remain united in our commitment to advancing conservative priorities, working with President Trump and our colleagues in the House, and holding the line against Democrats' efforts to enact their far-left, liberal agenda," Thune said in the statement.

"We have important work ahead of us, and I look forward to serving alongside my colleagues on behalf of the American people," he said.

#2867. Nissan cuts sales forecast again on China woes as CEO outlines plan to fight back

Nissan Motor plans to expand exports of vehicles developed in China to help compete against Chinese rivals globally, CEO Ivan Espinosa told CNBC on Wednesday.

The export push comes as intensifying competition in the world's largest auto market forced the company to cut its annual sales forecast for a second time in months.

Espinosa said Nissan would rapidly increase its exports of China-developed vehicles from two models to five, targeting 12 regional market clusters in the "very near term."

The Japanese automaker is responding to intensifying competition in China by developing vehicles specifically for Chinese consumers, using local technology, lower costs and faster development processes, he said.

"We can defend ourselves from these very aggressive players by using exactly the same elements that they have: speed, cost, and good technology," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Nissan faces weakening demand and an accelerating shift toward electric vehicles in China, even as its restructuring efforts show progress in the U.S. and Japan.

On Wednesday, Nissan cut its global sales outlook for the fiscal year ending March 2027 to between 2.97 million and 3 million vehicles, down from 3.15 million previously. It maintained its full-year operating profit and net cash guidance.

In China, "weaker demand, a faster-than-anticipated shift to [new energy vehicles] and intensifying competition are putting greater pressure on sales than initially anticipated," the company said in a statement.

In August, Nissan revised its full-year sales volume outlook from 3.3 million units to 3.15 million units.

In October, shares of peer Volvo Cars crashed after it reported a swing to operating loss, citing tough market conditions in China and the U.S.

CEO Håkan Samuelsson told CNBC at the time that it needed a new strategy in China, with regionally adapted cars.

"It's really a lot about the Chinese market, of course, where the change has come faster than we thought, and really now we can see that selling global cars developed in Europe and China will not work, and then this is affecting all the Western players in China," Samuelsson said.

On Wednesday, Nissan also reported a 96% drop in second-quarter operating profit to 2.1 billion yen ($13 million), significantly below analyst expectations of 54.9 billion yen, according to FactSet.

The drop in profits was due to no longer having the benefit of lower U.S. emissions-related expenses that boosted profits a year ago, the company said.

Net revenue grew 8.2% to 3.1 trillion yen in the quarter, largely in line with consensus.

The quarterly results came after the Tokyo market closed. Shares are down 29% year-to-date.

Nissan's Frankfurt-listed shares dropped 4% in early afternoon trading.

Espinosa, who has led the company since April 2025, also emphasised progress on a turnaround plan to reduce its global manufacturing footprint and cut its workforce by 15%. About 18 months into the plan, the company has achieved 365 billion yen in savings, Espinosa said.

#2865. Miami Heat Pivot to 2-Time All-Star as Kyrie Irving Trade Unlikely: Report

With Kyrie Irving and the Dallas Mavericks looking playoff-bound early in the season, the Miami Heat's odds of landing the NBA champion are bleak.

As such, Miami is expected to target Sacramento Kings All-Star Zach LaVine, who is reportedly on the trade block ahead of the Feb. 11 deadline.

According to NBA insider Ethan Skolnick, the Heat view LaVine — a career 20.7 PPG scorer — as the backcourt firepower needed to complement Bam Adebayo and Giannis Antetokounmpo while fixing their perimeter shooting and playmaking.

"I think they are looking at him, honestly," Skolnick said of LaVine, via Heat Central.

"I would watch the LaVine situation going forward. He's not Kyrie, but he'd give them another creator in the backcourt."

The Heat may not have much to lose by taking a gamble on LaVine, who is on an expiring $48.9M salary. Hypothetically, the Heat could package Andrew Wiggins with either Nikola Jovic or Bobby Portis to make the money work. If they wait until Dec. 15, the Heat can also use the contracts of Tim Hardaway Jr. and/or Nick Richards.

Erik Spoelstra's squad could desperately use some backcourt shot creation. Through 11 games, they rank 19th in offensive rating (111.3) and 22nd in 3P% (.336), lacking quality ball-handlers to complement or play off of Antetokounmpo. While Davion Mitchell has excelled in his role (16.5 PPG on 50% shooting), the Heat still lack reliable go-to options down the stretch — evidenced by a concerning 25th-ranked clutch FG% (.348), trailing five lottery-bound teams.

While Miami would still prefer to pair Irving with Antetokounmpo, Mavericks insider Tim MacMahon believes Dallas has zero intention of splitting up Irving and Cooper Flagg this season.

"You know, going into the season, I would have said more likely than not he gets moved by the deadline, but I didn't feel super strong about it," MacMahon told the "Hoop Collective" podcast last week, via Fadeaway World. "The more I see the team, the more I'm around the team, the more I think it's going to be awfully hard to trade him."

"Not that there won't be a big market, there will be, but if they've got a chance to compete for a play-in, playoff spot, I just think it's gonna be really tough to move him.

"Kyrie also, just as much as he means to that locker room, as well as he's working with Cooper, I mean, it was beautiful basketball. And, like, just basketball-wise, they are a phenomenal fit."

#2864. Takeaways from a drama-free blowout in the City of Brotherly Love

The Boston Celtics are 9-2, and 2-2 against teams that came into the season with realistic expectations of finishing at the top (or near the top) of their respective conferences. Boston's latest win, a 123-101 triumph over the Philadelphia 76ers, certainly came with a sizeable amount of fanfare and media attention.

Of course, that attention was inevitable. This was the first time Jaylen Brown was facing off against his former team since her was unceremoniously shipped out earlier this summer. It was also the first time we'd seen Jayson Tatum and Jaylen Brown go toe-to-toe in a competitive setting.

Just eleven games into the season, and we were already treated to a playoff-style game. Boston's ability to impose its will against a staple of the Sixers' defense gave it a leg up en route to an impressive win.

Before Tuesday's game against the Sixers, Philly was among the better teams in the NBA at limiting opposing teams' three-point attempts. Before the game against Boston, Cleaning The Glass had Philadelphia ranked 9th in the NBA for defensive three-point frequency.

More importantly, though, they were ranked third for defensive three-point percentage, limiting opponents to 31.8% from deep.

On the other side of this battle, the Celtics were coming into the game ranked sixth in both offensive three-point frequency and offensive three-point accuracy.

Simply put, the Sixers denied a lot of threes and held teams to a below-league-average clip on the threes they did allow. Boston takes a lot of threes (although not as many as in previous years) and makes a lot of them, too.

One of these two sides was going to lose this battle. Either the Sixers were going to implement their will and force the Celtics to beat them on the interior, or the Celtics were going to stare into the Sixers' souls, giggle, and make their shots anyway.

This is a Joe Mazzulla roster, so of course they chose violence, and giggle into the Sixers' souls they did.

Today, the Sixers have fallen from 9th in the NBA for defensive three-point frequency to 12th, and from third in the NBA for defensive three-point accuracy to eighth.

For the Celtics, their three-point shot is akin to their jab. They use it to size you up, to score points, and to set up the bigger blows. Philly came in looking to slip those jabs and hit the Celtics with the counterpunch in transition.

Instead, the Sixers allowed, well, more so witnessed, the Celtics having one of those nights where their threes are falling from everywhere. Boston ended the game shooting 44.9% from deep, on 22-of-49 shooting.

The Sixers got peppered with jabs, and in turn, the Sixers' biggest defensive strength looked penetrable. From there, the Celtics have a platform to build on, even when the whistle went cold against them.

That's how many Paul George scored in the fourth quarter. Eight more than anyone else on Boston's roster in the final twelve minutes of play, and two more than Anfernee Simons, who was the Sixers' only legitimate source of offense as they tried to keep pace with a Celtics team that surged out of the gates for the final round.

George has been way better than advertised to begin the season. Sure, there are still questions and/or concerns regarding his ability to stay healthy. Still, you can't deny the impact he's making when he steps onto the court for Boston, both offensively and defensively.

"I thought he was great," Joe Mazzulla said during his postgame news conference. "That's just what we need. We need him to play two-way basketball and be great. And you kind of saw that there, especially at the start of the fourth quarter."

George was a catalyst for the Celtics last night, in a game where his involvement in the Brown trade had become an afterthought.

That's how you remind people not to forget what you're capable of.

How many of us threw Sam Hauser's name into the trade machine this summer? How many times did you come across someone saying Boston needed to move on from him in order to strengthen other areas of the roster?

Hauser had another great night from the perimeter against the Sixers, going 4-of-6 from deep, along with snagging five rebounds and putting in a solid shift defensively.

This was just another game in a solid start to the season for Hauser. More importantly, he played a key role in unlocking the Sixers' perimeter defense early, hitting two of his four treys in the second quarter and proving that Philadelphia's point-of-attack defense wasn't the impenetrable wall the numbers would have you believe.

Hauser is knocking down threes at a 43.1% clip over his first 11 games of the season. If he keeps this up, it will be the best shooting season of his career since his rookie year. Of course, when you factor in volume (he took 1.7 threes per game as a rookie compared to 5.3 per game this season so far), I think it would be fair to say this is the best he's been as a straight-up perimeter bucket-getter.

Dean Wade and Brown split the lion's share of defensive responsibilities on Tatum. Wade totaled 3 minutes and 40 seconds paired up with Tatum, while Brown clocked 2 minutes and 30 seconds.

However, how each defender looked to play Boston's superstar was interesting. When Brown was matched up on Tatum, he was face guarding or top-locking, looking to freeze Tatum out of Boston's offense – which is a smart call. In fact, Tatum only took two shots with Brown as his on-ball defender, making both of them.

Now, part of the reason Brown didn't defend too many of Tatum's buckets is that the Celtics were more than happy to force Brown into a switch on the perimeter, as Tatum looked to punish Joel Embiid's mafia-style bricks for feet. For the most part, the Celtics were successful in hunting those switches, although sometimes that led to Tatum pairing up with VJ Edgecombe or Tyrese Maxey.

Either way, a quick pass to Tatum followed by a screen ensured we weren't seeing the two former teammates going at it possession after possession – as we did in 2023 when the pair were on opposite sides for the All-Star game.

Wade, on the other hand, appeared more than happy to guard Tatum on-ball and look to impact his scoring opportunities. In fact, Wade held Tatum to 1-of-5 shooting in their matchup, forcing Tatum into some tough spots, both on the perimeter and from two-point range.

When it comes to trying to shut down an elite talent like Tatum, you have to keep throwing things at the wall to see what sticks. Brown was undoubtedly motivated to pair up with his former running mate, but the truth is, placing Brown in an off-ball defensive role limits his value on that side of the ball. Boston knew that, which is what once Tatum got the rock in those matchup minutes, screens were only a few seconds away (mostly).

I'm sure that most of us are done with the constant Tatum and Brown reporting. We lived through it when they were teammates, and since Brown was traded, the media cycle has been relentless. Naturally, that reporting shifted into another gear heading into Tuesday's matchup.

I mean, it was a major storyline that added juice to a regular-season game in November; I get why that was the case. Fortunately, though, the game is over. Tatum and Brown have faced off. It's done. We saw it. It was cool. Now, can we please move on?

Actually, if I don't hear about the Tatum and Brown situation until we get closer to Brown's first return to the TD Garden (we're not counting a preseason game where he didn't play), then that would be fantastic.

It's time to move on. It's been time to move on. Hopefully, last night was the closure everybody needed to put this whole situation on pause and ultimately in the rearview.

No words, just the best highlight of the game.

For anyone looking to dine out on this win, I got some bad news for ya…The Celtics are back in action again tonight.

They're facing off against the 5-5 Toronto Raptors. As it's the second night of a back-to-back, it's fair to wonder whether Mitchell Robinson, George, or even Tatum will be present in the rotation. Right now, we don't know the answer to that. However, I'm sure it won't be long until the injury report is out and we see who, if anyone, is getting the night off to rest.

My problem with this matchup is that it has the potential to be a trap game. Scottie Barnes and Kawhi Leonard will not be participating. Immanuel Quickley is listed as doubtful, as is Collin Murray-Boyles.

When facing an injury-hit team, it's easy to think you can take your foot off the gas. In recent years, the Celtics haven't fallen victim to that mindset as much; in fact, it's been quite rare recently. Still, the risk is there, and when coupled with the tired legs from two games in two nights, it could make for a tough outing.

#2863. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2862. General strikes to paralyze Spain as protestors clash with police ahead of election campaign

Spanish workers are expected to turn out in the millions on Wednesday for the country's first general strike in 14 years, as anger over soaring rents and living costs intensifies less than three weeks before a general election. Demonstrators clashed with police at a wholesale market in Madrid in the early hours of the strike, while roadblocks were also reported in the early hours of Wednesday. The 24-hour labor walkout is expected to disrupt public transport and flights, as well as bring some businesses, shops and factories to a standstill.

Housing costs are a particular source of frustration among demonstrators. Rents have almost doubled over the past decade, squeezing household budgets as many workers feel homeownership is beyond their reach. The Bank of Spain estimates that the country faces a shortage of 750,000 homes, potentially rising to 1 million over the next two years. Spain's two largest trade unions, CCOO and UGT, which called the strike, are calling for greater government intervention in the housing market. Housing activists demand an end to tourist rentals and automatic lease renewals, while unions are seeking greater housing intervention and a new minimum-wage agreement.

UGT Secretary-General Pepe Álvarez urged members to join the protests, saying "there is no salary that can cope with the price of housing in this country," in comments translated by CNBC. He added that it was time for the government to listen to citizens' demands.

The strike follows weeks of social unrest. The eviction of an 87-year-old woman from her Madrid home, followed by her death around two weeks later, sparked national outrage. The left-wing government's attempts to pass measures to help tenants were blocked by the opposition.

Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez's coalition responded by calling an election for Nov. 29, hoping to bolster his support and secure another four years in power. But a high-speed train collision that killed 69 people, the second such disaster this year, shifted the debate toward public safety and accusations of government mismanagement.

The government has not officially endorsed the strike, whose slogan is "Salaries. Housing. Prices." But the protests are widely seen as an opportunity for left-wing parties, including Sánchez's Spanish Socialist Workers' Party, to put housing at the center of the election campaign. Political scientist Oriol Bartomeus of the Autonomous University of Barcelona told The Associated Press that a strong, peaceful turnout could help the left return the campaign to a focus on housing. Sánchez took to social media earlier this week to voice support for existing policies including national health insurance and a 40-hour workweek, warning that a right-wing government would introduce austerity measures and cuts to social services.

"Your eight-hour working day. Your pensions. Your public healthcare. None of this fell from the sky. They were collective achievements," he told voters on Monday.

The conservative Popular Party and far-right Vox, which already govern in some parts of Spain, accuse the unions of organizing the protests to keep the current government in power. "The strike is not a general strike, it's an election strike," Miguel Tellado, a spokesperson for the Popular Party, said Tuesday.

Both parties oppose intervention in the housing market.

Opinion polls suggest a shift to the right, with the Popular Party leading and potentially able to form a government with Vox.

Such a coalition would bring the far right into Spain's national government for the first time since Francisco Franco's 36-year dictatorship ended in 1975.

A poll for El País and Cadena SER, published Nov. 9, put the Popular Party at 31.9%, Sánchez's Partido Socialista Obrero Español at 29.9% and Vox at 18%. The survey, conducted Nov. 2-4, projected a combined 190 seats for the Popular Party and Vox, above the 176 needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. A strong turnout at Wednesday's protests could prove crucial for the government, whose handling of the migration crisis in Ceuta drew widespread criticism. Sánchez's family has also been plagued by corruption scandals.

The prime minister's personal standing has suffered. Some 58% of respondents had a "bad" or "very bad" opinion of him, compared with 41% who had a good or very good opinion, according to a poll for elDiario.es conducted Nov. 2-5.

Violence during the strike, however, could backfire on the government. Protests in France in October were seen as an opportunity for the country's far-right party to emphasize its ability to restore order and security ahead of the country's 2027 presidential elections.

"On the evening of 11 November, there will almost certainly be two narratives: one of resounding success and one of failure. Neither should be read as an exit poll. It will be more useful to look at other things: which sectors and regions stop work, how many people take to the streets and, above all, whether housing moves to the centre of the campaign," wrote Maite Aurrekoetxea, professor of social and human sciences at the University of Deusto, in comments translated by Google.
