#2861. Is Trent Williams enjoying a career year at age 38?

The San Francisco 49ers are rolling at 7-1, coming off a big 38-17 win over the Las Vegas Raiders last weekend, as they set up for the second half of the year.

San Francisco's offense has been the story, with Brock Purdy being a top MVP candidate, while the 49ers rank third in points per game. Mike Evans has been a godsend for the passing attack, but San Francisco has also been running the ball much more efficiently this year.

Christian McCaffrey and Kaelon Black are both averaging 4.5 yards per carry, while the team ranks seventh in the NFL with 135.3 rushing yards per game. The 49ers have also been the seventh-most efficient team, averaging 4.6 yards per carry on the ground.

From an EPA perspective, the 49ers are third in EPA per rush and first in rushing success rate. They've completely flipped things around from the 2025 season, where the run game was a bit of a struggle, even with a healthy Christian McCaffrey.

A big part of the success has been 38-year-old star left tackle Trent Williams. The 49ers are averaging 5.9 yards running behind the left tackle and 4.8 yards to the left end behind Williams. Then, there's the pass protection, which has been a big strength for the 49ers this year.

Remember, the 49ers didn't allow a sack over the first four weeks of the season. They've allowed just eight on the whole year. Williams has allowed just eight pressures and one sack this season, while being fully healthy at 38 years old.

The superstar left tackle has enjoyed a number of extremely good seasons in the NFL, with the 2021 campaign being the most notable. Since joining the 49ers in 2020, he's been a three-time First Team All-Pro, while also earning five Pro Bowl nods and one Second Team All-Pro honor.

And yet, this could be his best season yet, despite him being 38 years old. Williams was ESPN's Protector of the Year a quarter way through the season in Bill Barnwell's awards. And he feels like the clear frontrunner for the award at the midway point.

What has Kyle Shanahan seen from his left tackle this season?

"He's playing damn good," Shanahan said on Wednesday. "I mean, stat-wise, probably statistically and things like that, I'd say yes, [it's the best year of his career]. He's playing at a real high level. It's hard to compare Pro Bowl years to other Pro Bowl years, but he's playing at a very high level."

Last week, offensive coordinator Klay Kubiak was asked about Williams's excellence at 38 years old, and he broke down how rare of a player the star left tackle was.

"Yeah, I don't know how to explain it," Kubiak said of Williams's level of play at his age. "It's why he's the best. Trent's got his way of preparing. A way of getting his body ready and his mind ready to play football games every week that's unlike anyone I've been around. The way that he goes out there on Wednesdays and he practices, and he goes through his routine. He gets himself ready to play, and he knows what it takes to get himself ready.

"He's continuing to play at a very, very high level. To me, he's still the best offensive lineman in the league, and to me, that's a credit to his process and the way he approaches the game mentally and his love of the game. He's a great teammate. Just all around, everything he does to get ready to play football games and to be the best for his team, is exceptional. To me, it's what makes him so rare."

Williams is likely reaching retirement at some point over the next few years. But you'd never be able to guess that from his level of play this season.

#2860. Tired of spending time fixing other people's AI slop at work? How to talk to them about it

You open slides you got from a teammate for a joint project presentation you're giving. Within minutes you suspect that it's largely AI-generated. The stats are incorrect and the writing is littered with generic phrases that sound plausible at a glance but, upon inspection, say nothing of substance.

AI was supposed to make work faster and easier, and sometimes it does. Eighty-five percent of surveyed workers, all of whom were active AI users, said the technology saved them between one and seven hours per week, according to a Workday study of 3,200 full-time employees published in January.

But as the author of "Managing Up" and an executive coach to high performers at companies like Google, Amazon and JPMorgan, I've seen a new problem emerge. You may regularly find yourself on the receiving end of what looks like finished work that still needs hours of editing. The Workday survey found that nearly 40% of the time "saved" from using AI was then lost to "rework" like verifying, correcting and rewriting.

Calling that extra work out to your colleagues and bosses can feel awkward. You might use AI too; 40.5% of employed U.S. adults ages 18 to 64 reported using AI for work in Q3 2026, according to data from the Real-Time Population Survey. So you don't want to sound hypocritical. Plus, you can't always be 100% sure someone used AI, even if it seems obvious to you.

So how do you address the extra work landing on your plate without the conversation becoming confrontational?

Begin by showing that you're not trying to embarrass anyone or prove them wrong. You're on the same side.

Frame your feedback in terms of working toward an outcome that preserves the quality of the work and the team's reputation. That way, your intentions are clear, which makes the discussion less adversarial from the outset.

Avoid saying, "this whole thing looks AI-generated," especially if you can't prove that. A blanket accusation takes the focus off of what needs to change and puts the other person on the defensive.

Instead, point to concrete issues such as vague wording, missing context, hallucinated examples or off-brand visuals.

This is particularly important when speaking with your boss or someone senior, since you don't want to suggest that you're judging their choices.

It can help to acknowledge if you use AI too, which makes it clear that the extra work and the impression the report or presentation could make are the problems, not the tool itself.

Be specific about how much time it's already required or will involve to clean up the work and whether it's taken away from other priorities. The other person may not realize the cost unless you explicitly say it.

Depending on the relationship, you might ask them to review AI-generated content before sending it over, limit when AI is used or simply flag it up front so you can plan accordingly.

Remember, your colleagues and leaders are trying to work faster and keep up with ever-growing demands, just like you are. When you push back without shaming them, you show that you respect their intentions while still protecting your time.

Melody Wilding, LMSW is an executive coach, human behavior professor and author of "Managing Up: How to Get What You Need from the People in Charge." Get her free training, 5 Steps to Speak Like a Senior Leader, here.

#2859. What centers could the Lakers target on the trade market?

The Lakers are riding a high right now, winners of six in a row and sitting atop the league looking down. But there's always the question of how it can get even better.

This week's mailbag saw questions based on potential trades for backup centers as well as prospects to watch with Coachella Valley in the G League.

The backup center position is going to be arguably the top priority for the Lakers between now and the trade deadline. While the Jarred Vanderbilt-Sandro Mamukelashvili pairing has done a suitable job as a short-term solution, it doesn't feel like one that will work when the games really matter.

The pairing offers spacing offensively, but can they hold up enough defensively in the playoffs? And if they don't, then who can you target that will?

I still worry about Brook Lopez defensively in the playoffs because the last time we saw him in the postseason, it was really rough. But depending on the price, there could be value there.

Similarly, Jericho Sims will likely remain a name on the Lakers' radar until the deadline. He has played in six games this season, but he has played more than eight minutes only twice. Do they try to get really frisky and try to get Kel'el Ware from Milwaukee, another player they were linked to? The asking price will probably be too high, but it'll be worth a question.

There could be a buyout market to find someone, but it's hard to determine what that'll look like in November.

As for Looney, I think he brings value as a leader and on this team. He's been around the block more than anyone else. And there are a couple other guys I'd have higher on the list to waive before him.

I don't think this start really changes the team's approach at the trade deadline. For one, the team is still going to prioritize having the ability to use the mid-level exception next season, so I can't imagine they'll take on long-term contract without sending out long-term money.

PJ Washington, for example, would cut into that room next season. Daniel Gafford, who I did not mention in the last section, creates similar issues depending on the outgoing players. It's why I predict they'll make a small move, then use their MLE in the summer and look to move an expiring Vando contract for another piece.

Coachella Valley has now played three games this season. Chris Mañon had a career-high 32 points in the second game while Cole Swider had 22 and 21 points in the first two games.

For now, Mañon and Kaluma are the obvious players to watch for obvious reasons. AK Okereke returned to Coachella Valley, so he's another one to look out for. William Kyle was an interesting prospect in Summer League and could be a sort of Kaluma-esque candidate for a breakout season after a year in the G League.

As for Swider, I get the rationale of wanting to play younger players with more potential, but I also think there's value in simply having talented players on the court. Swider is your typical AAAA player who won't stick around in the NBA but will be good in the G League.

Having him around helps space the floor for Kaluma or Mañon to drive or gives Kyle room to operate as an athletic finisher. He gives Mañon a target to find coming off screens. He complements the players on the roster, which is valuable. Having a handful of guys like that on the roster isn't a bad thing, plus he's a player the organization is familiar with, too.

This is probably a longer piece that we may dive into at some point, but the teams have changed quite a bit.

One of the biggest differences is that the March 2026 Lakers were buoyed by finishing at a very high level on drives to the rim. They were also a different team defensively as one that didn't create as much chaos but also had a better defensive rating, partially because they could clean the glass to end defensive possessions.

This team's defense is built around turning teams over and blocking shots. They're still working on the ending possessions with a rebound part, though. Offensively, they're a more prolific and efficient offense from range so far.

NEW YORK, NEW YORK - JULY 06: Tim Hill #41 of the New York Yankees throws a pitch during the eighth inning against the New York Mets at Citi Field on July 06, 2025 in New York City. (Photo by Justin Casterline/Getty Images) Getty

The New York Mets are set to have a very busy offseason. Top exec David Stearns is looking to revamp his roster after yet another disappointing season. According to the writer's sources, the Mets are targeting some of the most highly rated left-handed relievers on the market. GettyTim Hill has faced the New York Mets 22 times throughout his MLB career.

The New York Mets only had two prominent southpaws in their bullpen this past season: David Peterson and Brooks Raley. Both were traded away in the summer.

Interestingly, Hill is not the only former Yankees pitcher to be recently linked to the New York Mets. Paul Blackburn was also recently projected to sign with the Queens club during the offseason. Both relievers featured heavily for the Yankees throughout the most recent campaign. Blackburn was previously an All-Star in 2022 with the Athletics.

The Mets, however, will likely have other targets as well. Stearns and Co. could even target a trade if certain free agents sign elsewhere. Either way, New York will be adding relief pitchers in the near future. Stearns even told reporters on November 10 (as reported by Sammon) that the Mets will soon add to their bullpen.

#2857. Rams targeting Davante Adams to return in Week 13 vs. Chiefs; Puka Nacua to miss 'significant' time

Sean McVay delivered a mixed bag of news Wednesday regarding injuries to the team's top two receivers.

Davante Adams could be back sooner than expected from a hamstring injury. Puka Nacua, meanwhile, will remain sidelined for a "significant period of time" with knee and toe injuries.

McVay announced the updates while speaking with reporters. Per McVay, Adams is on track to return on the early end of his 6-10 week injury timeline and could be back as soon as Week 13 for a showdown against the Kansas City Chiefs.

"We're optimistic Davante's return is sooner than later, which is a big deal to us," McVay said, per the Associated Press.

McVay went on to say that a return for Adams after Week 12 against the Packers would be "a realistic timeline for us."

If that timeline holds true, that means Adams will miss two more games with a ruptured hamstring that he sustained in Week 6 against the Arizona Cardinals. The Rams face the Cardinals again on Sunday, then have a Week 11 bye before facing the Packers in Week 12.

An early return would be big for a Rams team that's currently down its top two weapons in the passing game.

Nacua sustained sprains to the MCL and PCL in his right knee and a turf toe injury in Sunday's win over the Washington Commanders. The Rams announced Monday that they expected to place the All-Pro receiver on injured reserve with the injuries.

McVay said Wednesday that Nacua won't go on injured reserve, but only because of "some semantics with the roster."

"He's still going to be out for a significant period of time," McVay said.

What that timeline is precisely remains unclear, but McVay indicated that it could be at or beyond the minimum four-game absence required by placement on injured reserve.

Davante Adams, left, is expected to return sooner from injury than Puka Nacua.

In total, Wednesday's news was encouraging for the Rams. Nacua was expected to miss significant time, and nothing there has changed. That the Rams could get Adams back for the stretch run of the playoff race is a win.

The long end of his initial timeline would have placed him back for Week 17 and the last two games of the regular season. If he returns against the Chiefs and stays healthy, he'd be available for the last six games of the season, providing Matthew Stafford with a top-shelf target in the passing game.

A six-time Pro Bowler and three-time All-Pro, Adams was putting up Pro Bowl numbers prior to his injury. In six games, he tallied 30 catches for 480 yards and 4 touchdowns. If he returns in Week 13, he'll do so as the clear No. 1 option in the passing game as long as Nacua remains sidelined.

The Rams need all the help they can get as they face a fight just to make the playoffs. With Sunday's win, they improved to 5-4 for their first winning record of the season. But it's not good enough to put them on the right side of the postseason picture.

If the playoffs started this week, they'd be the ninth-place team in an NFC field in which seven teams make the postseason.

The Rams have a path to the postseason that's bolstered by the return of Myles Garrett, who tallied three sacks and a forced fumble against Washington in his first game back from knee surgery.

Their hope is that they can make the field with Garrett, Nacua and Adams healthy as a threat to win the Super Bowl.

#2856. American soldier arrested on suspicion of spying for Russia

A senior soldier in the U.S. Army has been arrested in the U.K. on suspicion of spying for Russia, London's Metropolitan Police said on Tuesday evening.

The unnamed 41-year-old man was arrested near Gloucester, a city in western England, on Tuesday as part of an investigation by the U.K.'s Counter Terrorism Policing. British police worked with U.S. authorities, including the military and the FBI, in their investigations.

A senior NATO military official confirmed on Tuesday that the American service member was assigned to the military alliance's Allied Rapid Reaction Corps (ARRC).

ARRC, headquartered in the English county of Gloucestershire, is a  3-star NATO headquarters with more than 400 permanent staff from more than 20 nations ready to deploy land forces where they are needed.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters on Wednesday that Moscow had "absolutely nothing to do with" the case in the U.K.

The individual at the center of the case, detained in London, is suspected of committing offenses under Sections 1 and 3 of the U.K.'s National Security Act.

Section 1 offenses relate to obtaining or disclosing protected information, while Section 3 involves assisting a foreign intelligence service.

The maximum penalty is life imprisonment for a Section 1 offense and 14 years' imprisonment for a Section 3 offense. A fine may also be imposed for either offense.

Police officers are carrying out searches at two locations in Gloucestershire, the Metropolitan Police added.

The arrest comes as Washington is seeking to broker an end to Russia's war in Ukraine. U.S.-mediated talks between Russian and Ukrainian officials began in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday, the first such meeting since February, while Kyiv and its European allies continue to press for credible security guarantees as part of any settlement.

President Donald Trump, a longstanding critic of NATO and its members' defense budgets, said on Monday that there appeared to be a growing willingness on both sides to end the almost five-year war.

Russia has long threatened retaliatory action against an expansion of NATO's eastern flank and has previously demanded guarantees that Ukraine will never be allowed to join the alliance. Finland and Sweden became NATO's newest members in 2023 and 2024, respectively.

Since Russia and Ukraine have been at war, Russian activity has also been reported deeper into European and NATO territory, with regional authorities investigating whether Moscow could be involved in some of the recent disarray seen across the continent.

FBI Director Kash Patel said in a statement on Tuesday evening that the allegations against a U.S. serviceman were "of the utmost concern to the United States and our allies."

"I want to send a clear message – this FBI has no tolerance for anyone who breaks their oath to the United States and whose actions endanger our national security and that of our partners," he said.

Sean Stinchon, the commanding general of U.S. Army Counterintelligence Command, said in a statement that "the alleged conduct of a senior solider, entrusted with sensitive information, is a sobering reminder that insider threats are real and persistent."

Authorities said Tuesday that the investigation is not related to any other ongoing Counter Terrorism Policing investigations.

On Oct. 27, British prosecutors said they had charged a man with assisting a foreign intelligence service over an incident at a military base used by the U.S. for strikes on Iran. The base in question, RAF Fairford, is in Gloucestershire, around 30 miles from ARRC's headquarters at Imjin Barracks.

#2855. Lions Coach Gives Troubling Update on Two Starters

Detroit Lions head coach Dan Campbell delivered a motivating message when asked about the team's emotional state after Jack Campbell's injury. Getty

The Detroit Lions were without starters Cade Mays, D.J. Reed, and D.J. Wonnum in Week 9. Head coach Dan Campbell admitted that while he expects Wonnum to return after passing concussion protocol, he does not expect Reed to return from his hip injury or Mays from his ankle.

"Wonnum, he's cleared his first obstacle," said Campbell on Wonnum passing concussion protocol. "Second obstacle here. So he's going to be able to practice today; as long as he gets through practice, he should be good to go."

He was not nearly as optimistic about Mays and Reed.

"I don't see Cade and Reed practicing today," stated Campbell. "They've probably got a way outside shot of plan, way, way, way outside shot. They could."

Campbell was asked if both would travel with the team to Germany. He made it clear that they would, but their status for the game on Sunday was up in the air.

"I honestly, for both of them, it's really more about, do we feel like they could play or not play, more than the travel for them," said Campbell.

Mays has missed the past two weeks. Reed missed his first game in Week 9.

GettyDetroit Lions head coach Dan Campbell.

The biggest loss will be Reed. Reed has been the Lions' best cornerback in an unstable cornerback room. Without him, they have four different options that they rotate. Rock Ya-Sin mixes in with Khalil Dorsey and Ennis Rakestraw. Roger McCreary mans the slot in nickel situations and moves to the outside in base.

Between the four of them, they have depth, but do not have a starter as strong as Reed. Detroit could have added a cornerback at the NFL trade deadline, but stayed put. They added a safety, and if Kerby Joseph comes back, they could lean into safety Brian Branch in the slot.

Then, McCreary could play on the outside more.

Mays was expected to be a notable free agent signing to start at center. However, he injured his wrist and missed the first five games. He returned in Week 7 against the Green Bay Packers and struggled. Then, he got hurt again in Week 8. He missed Week 9 and is trending towards missing Week 10.

The issue with Mays is that the team gave him a notable contract in free agency. Soon-to-be free agent Juice Scruggs has been the better and more reliable option for the team.

So, they are not missing out on Mays. However, losing Mays does set up a bigger question in the offseason. The Lions play the New England Patriots in Germany in Week 10.

#2854. Zelenskyy warns Ukraine is 'not real estate' as experts say a Russia peace deal remains elusive

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy warned that his country "was not real estate," as a second and final day of U.S.-brokered peace talks wrapped up.

Speaking to reporters in Denmark late Tuesday, Zelenskyy said he was "happy" with what he was hearing from the peace talks in Abu Dhabi between Russian and Ukrainian representatives, but said he had been clear with U.S. envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner that "this is a country of our nation, it is not real estate."

His comments come amid discussions on the future borders of Ukraine and post-war security guarantees, issues where the countries remain far apart.

"Everybody is ready for dialog. First, stop the war in Ukraine. Second, security guarantees for Ukraine. Third, maybe some dialog about these topics of security," Zelenskyy said. "This sequence is very, very important."

It comes as the first trilateral peace talks between Russia, Ukraine and the U.S. since February concluded on Wednesday.

Speaking post-meeting, Kushner called the talks "productive" and said the parties had also discussed the difficult issue of territorial boundaries. The Ukrainian negotiator meanwhile said momentum was good and a solution could be reached before the end of the year.

Russia chief negotiator and Putin aide Vladimir Medinsky said the parties on Wednesday had reached an agreement on a large-scale prisoner exchange, and that details would be announced soon.

Another round of exchanges of prisoners of war would be welcomed, Mykola Bielieskov, senior analyst at Ukrainian NGO Come Back Alive, told CNBC Tuesday. "Otherwise there is no room for compromise," he said, adding, "I don't have any expectations of major progress towards settlement."

According to Bielieskov, Ukraine could accept a ceasefire along the current front lines, but would never formally recognize Russian sovereignty over occupied territory. He argued that Zelenskyy has neither the legal authority nor public support to surrender Ukrainian land, even if Kyiv acknowledges it cannot reclaim those territories militarily in the near term.

Russia's demands for additional territory, restrictions on NATO membership and limits on Ukraine's armed forces remain fundamental obstacles to a deal, he added.

Russia has publicly maintained its demand that Ukraine surrender the entire eastern Donbas region, including territory Moscow has not captured.

While Ukraine and European allies push for a ceasefire as an initial step, before moving on to issues such as security guarantees, borders and reconstruction, Russia has signaled it considers security guarantees necessary before ending fighting.

"It looks like because Russia has objected to a ceasefire proposal that Ukraine and the U.S. earlier last year made, the Trump administration is no longer trying to achieve a ceasefire, even though that's logically the first step," former U.S. ambassador William Courtney told CNBC early Tuesday.

Courtney said Russia has shown no signs of seriously pursuing a ceasefire in Ukraine, despite Kyiv's willingness to freeze the conflict along current battle lines.

He argued that Moscow's territorial demands and continued attacks suggest its objectives remain unchanged, making meaningful negotiations unlikely without a shift in Russia's position or battlefield conditions.

Maximilian Hess at political risk firm Enmetena Advisory told CNBC's Dan Murphy Wednesday, ahead of the second day of talks, that little has been achieved in the latest round of meetings so far.

While Zelenskyy has urged leaders to speak directly, Hess said it's unlikely the Ukrainian leader will be willing to meet with Putin without meaningful concessions from Russia, given the risk of domestic backlash.

He argued that Putin could use a potential meeting with Trump and Xi Jinping in China to seek further U.S. sanctions relief, weaken Washington's support for Ukraine, and deepen divisions within the West.

On a potential Zelenskyy-Putin meeting, Kushner said the Russian position is that "they'd like to see more progress made before that happens".

#2853. Brooklyn Nets vs. New Orleans Pelicans preview: Searching for a win

The team was never really in it after the first quarter and got trucked, losing 130-93. You've got to give people something worth holding on to at some point.

Speaking of things being worth a shot, we might get our first extended look at Egor Demin and Mikel Brown Jr playing together. So far this year, they've only been limited to four minutes together on the court, but that should change in a hurry. Brooklyn's guards have shown glimpses of excellence thus far, and we'll see how the vision for them continues to develop as the season progresses.

On a team level, they have to be more careful with the ball. Too many of their games have gotten out of control due to ill timed turnovers, inability to rebound, etc.

"I know the guys are not trying to turn it over, so put less pressure on yourself when you make those decisions," Jordi Fernández said. "It's more about our spacing and then how we do from there because we're pretty unorganized early and then that doesn't help. So I don't want them to play tentative; I want them to play aggressive, and making their decisions and playing very hard.

Fortunately for the Nets, they are playing a Pelicans team that is allowing 116.8 points per 100 possessions, fifth worst in the NBA. Unfortunately for Brooklyn, they allow 114.8 points per 100 possessions, seventh worst in the NBA. This one figures to be sloppy and frantic.

I think two things are true when discussing Zion Williamson. The first is that he's had what we can consider a successful career. He's gotten to the All-Star game a few times, has made over $150 million and counting, and is likely due to cash in on another big contract before his career ends. This season, he's averaging 21 points on a career best 62.8 percent from the field and has taken some more three pointers than you'd expect out of him.

It's also true that we expected a LOT more from the former number one pick. Numerous issues and controversies have dulled his impact in the league and instead of being a transcendent star, is merely a good player and potential trade chip this winter. If you told me eight years ago that's how things would go for him, I would have called you a liar. But alas.

Williamson would be helping himself immensely if he were a better rebounder. He's never been great at it and his rebound rate is at career lows to start the season. From Chris Lambert at Pelicans Debrief:

"There is zero reason for a player with his frame and athleticism to not be jumping up and snatching boards like they're going out of style. Then you factor in he still gets lazy defensively and lets his man blow by him way too often, and I ask: what value do his defensive playmaking numbers even bring?

Honestly, I would rather he average one steal and block zero shots but give this team six-plus rebounds and actually provide a consistent effort when guarding on the ball. That would provide more value for this group than starting a few fast breaks. Well, actually, maybe not because the half-court is putrid, so maybe I take that back."

Another lefty All-Star will look to put a good outing on the board. Like Williamson, Julius Randle has to be the best player on the court in order for his team to win. It hasn't happened enough this year and it makes life more challenging for his teammates. Not that single-game plus minus tells the entire story, but the Nets were -33 in Randle's minutes on Sunday. He's the guy who's out there the most and the player the team expects the most out of. With an extra day-off to recuperate and fine tune some things, Fernandez is hoping Randle gets off to a strong start and builds on it throughout the evening.

So we got word that former World Championship Wrestling executive Jim Herd passed away a few months ago. Herd's time in charge wasn't especially good, but during his run, we did get one of the greatest matches of all time, so let's watch that!

#2851. WNBA Finals MVP Gabby Williams: 'Our existence is political'

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

Golden State Valkyries star Gabby Williams doesn't expect the political and cultural issues swirling around the WNBA to go away anytime soon.

Fresh off the Valkyries' first championship and winning the Finals MVP award, Williams spoke with CNBC's Alex Sherman about the league's place in those conversations. The WNBA has been at the forefront of several political and cultural issues this season, ranging from race to sexuality.

"I think as a female athlete, just our existence is political," Williams said. "Being a female athlete just automatically becomes politicized because we're in a space where maybe we weren't welcome before."

Williams said that she and many of her fellow athletes understand the fact that that playing professional basketball has brought them a larger platform.

"I think it's something that we embrace as athletes," Williams said, pointing to players' past involvement in political and social causes, including the 2020 Georgia Senate runoff and the Black Lives Matter movement. "I think that'll continue," she said.

The 30-year-old basketball player, born in Nevada to an American father and a French mother, said she grew up watching the Black Lives Matter movement while a student-athlete at the University of Connecticut.

"Realizing, oh, wow, these aren't just basketball players. They're leaders in our community. I was actually looking forward to that part of the W," she said.

Williams has used her platform to speak out in support of transgender rights, a topic that became a flashpoint for the league this season after Indiana Fever player Sophie Cunningham argued that transgender women should not compete in women's sports.

Williams has maintained that sports should be open to everyone, and she's revealed that's she gotten hateful messages and death threats because of her views.

Williams said the Valkyries’ home arena, the Chase Center, which has become known as "Ballhalla" for its electric atmosphere, helped her feel welcome and gave her the confidence to thrive this season.

"Being in Ballhalla allowed me to be myself," Williams said. "As soon as I got there, I felt so welcomed. In the preseason game, I didn't even play, and people were like screaming for me and welcoming me. And it just gives me a confidence, that I think I can only get from Ballhalla."

#2849. Game Preview: Knicks vs. Cavaliers, November 11, 2026

Remember when the Knicks started 0-3, and people thought that the sky was falling? Turns out talks of the team no longer being good were exaggerated after all.

New York has now won six of their last seven games and are now winners of four straight after Tuesday night's thrashing of a Bucks team that has been maybe even worse than expected. Part of that can and should be attributed to a lighter schedule over the last couple of weeks. The Knicks have played the 1-8 Bulls twice, the 2-8 Nets, and the aforementioned 1-10 Bucks. Of their two quality wins during that stretch, one was against the 7-3 Pistons and the other came against the Cavaliers, the latter perhaps not that great a deal given the interesting season they've had and the fact they were playing on the end of a back-to-back.

Up until the Bulls game last week, I would've called the Knicks' season a roller coaster ride. But whatever early-season turbulence and disappointments the Knicks experienced, the Cavaliers might have surpassed it. At 5-6, they are just 1.5 games behind the Knicks. And it's only been 11 games. But the Cavaliers have lost two in a row, have lost four of five and five of seven.

A lot of it starts with the starting lineup, which is ironic given the ceaseless talks surrounding the Knicks' very own starters. Coming into Wednesday night, the Cavaliers' preferred starting lineup of James Harden, Donovan Mitchell, Peyton Watson, Evan Mobley, and Jarrett Allen has an abysmal net rating of -18.9 in 84 minutes together. That number is a lot better when one of Mobley, Watson, or Allen is off the court, with the net ratings in those minutes all being positive, suggesting that the talent is there, but the synergy may not be.

There are three players in particular who deserve a closer look. Harden is currently averaging 15.3PPG and 5.7APG both of which are the third and fourth lowest of his career, respectively. He's currently shooting just 35.5% from three, 37.5% from two, and shooting just 4.8FTA/G. Given how little he provides as a defender, he's been a tough watch this season, and the numbers back it up. In the 359 minutes with Harden on the floor, the Cavaliers have had a -6.0 net rating. In the 174 minutes with him off the floor, they have a +16.5 net rating. That's a 22.5-point swing.

He isn't the only one that has been a disappointment, though. Watson, the Cavaliers' major off-season trade acquisition, was looked to be the athletic shooting wing with size that could finally lock down the small forward position. Critics of the move were cautious given how much he benefitted from Nikola Jokic's gravity and passing, but also given his relatively small sample size of being the kind of player the Cavaliers were hoping to get. While it's unfair to judge Watson on just nine games in a new city and situation, he's been a subpar player. Watson is currently averaging just 11.4PPG and 4.6RPG while having an assist-to-turnover ratio of 2.7:1.8 and is shooting a putrid 21.6% from three and barely hovering above 40% from the field. And like Harden, he's been a negative when on the floor. The Cavaliers have a -5.2 net rating in the 250 minutes with him on the floor. Some of that may simply be due to the fact that he has to share a large chunk of his minutes with Harden. But it also doesn't fully explain why the Cavaliers have a +7.1 net rating in the 283 minutes without him or why the starting lineup sans Watson actually has a +30.4 net rating in 23 minutes.

Maybe the most interesting case study, though, is Mobley. His individual stats look fine for the most part. He's averaging 17.5PPG and 7.5RPG while shooting 55.8% from the field and 50% from three. But the Cavaliers are a -2.4 in his minutes. And even when he shares the floor with Mitchell and Allen, the trio is a -20.5 in 87 minutes. Meanwhile, the duo of Mitchell and Allen without Mobley is a whopping +26.1 in 67 minutes with Mitchell being a +7.0 in his minutes and Allen being a +5.0 in his.

The team can still turn things around. As Knicks fans were told to do after 144 regular season minutes, Cavaliers fans should be reminded to relax because it really is a marathon. But as you can see, there are some very clear holes in this team. Some have pointed to Kenny Atkinson's inability to fully get this group to gel. I do think there is some validity to that. He's either not making the requisite changes or his players aren't listening. And either can be a fireable offense. But he can't bear all of the blame.

The team has very little depth. And at the end of the day, it comes down to his players simply playing better. Harden can't shoot this bad if he's going to give close to zero effort defensively. Watson needs to shoot better. And Mobley, despite putting together a solid campaign statistically, needs to continue becoming much more of an aggressive force both offensively and defensively.

All that being said, the Knicks are in prime position to extend their winning streak to five. They took care of business against the lowly Bucks last night, which allowed their starters to rest. Not a single player played more than 29 minutes, so they should be ready tonight. It can be difficult to beat a team twice in such a short span, but given the Cavaliers' recent struggles and how well the Knicks have played them as of late, New York should be favored.

When you play a team with expectations, such as the Cavaliers, that has underperformed the way they have, you run the risk of running into a hungry and sometimes even angry team. Cleveland has the talent on paper to beat anybody on any given night. But we saw last Saturday that this team doesn't have a single player that can defend Jalen Brunson or Karl-Anthony Towns, and they just do not have the depth, especially at the center position, to reliably stay physical with a team like the Knicks.

With New York finally finding their groove and Cleveland slipping, I expect a closer game than the last one, but the Knicks to hold them off, improving to 7-4.

Who: New York Knicks (6-4) vs. Cleveland Cavaliers (5-6)Date: Wednesday, November 11, 2026Time: 7:00 PM ETPlace: Madison Square Garden, New York, NYTV: ESPNFollow: @ptknicksblog and bsky

#2847. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2846. Blowout loss to Celtics exposed the Sixers’ two biggest issues

The Sixers got their asses handed to them by the Celtics on Tuesday night, to put it bluntly. They fell into a double-digit hole early in the second quarter, managed to claw their way back to tie the game late in the third, then got buried by an avalanche of three-pointers and called it a night.

While the beef and/or lamb chops and gravy between Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum took center stage Tuesday night, the real story of the game was that the Sixers and Celtics came into it with the two best records in the Eastern Conference. This was the first of four matchups between two legitimate title hopefuls, one with no shortage of bad blood on either side (as much as the Celtics try to deny it).

The result raised two glaring red flags for the Sixers, ones which have been prevalent throughout this early start to the season. Their lack of three-point shooting and lack of rim protection behind Joel Embiid once again came back to bite them, and this time, they weren't able to muster a second-half rally via lockdown defense.

Through their first 12 games, the Sixers are 24th in the league in made threes per game, 26th in three-point attempts and 21st in three-point percentage. In the 22-point loss to Boston, they got outscored by 33 points from deep.

The Celtics have been one of the NBA's highest-volume three-point shooting teams under Joe Mazzulla, and that hasn't changed this year. After Tuesday's avalanche of triples, they're now second in the league in three-point makes, fifth in attempts and third in long-range shooting percentage.

Even though the Sixers drew 28 free-throw attempts compared to only five for the Celtics, they weren't able to overcome the math advantage from Boston's three-point barrage.

"We weren't aggressive enough defensively, and they got loose," Tyrese Maxey said after the game. "For them to get up 50 threes is, you know… the way they got them up, too. The catch-and-shoot threes, the open threes. We know their game plan is to get up threes. But we can't give up the catch-and-shoot threes to this team because the result is this right here."

Three-point shooting loomed as a legitimate concern for the Sixers heading into the season. Maxey is the only plus long-range shooter in their starting lineup. All four of their other starters can knock down triples as well, but they aren't particularly reliable from deep. Opponents will happily concede those shots, particularly if they're contested, if that allows them to wall off the paint and prevent easy looks at the basket.

The one silver lining to Tuesday's game is that Joel Embiid, who was mired in a brutal 0-of-19 three-point slump over his previous seven games, finally knocked one down at the beginning of the second half.

"I think you can see it, as soon as I made a three, the next shot that I had in the short corner on the right side, I didn't even think about it, I just shot it, let it fly. And I made it," Embiid said after the game. "Probably if I hadn't made a three, I probably would have been in my mind and would have thought about it."

Head coach Nick Nurse doesn't seem particularly concerned about the Sixers' three-point woes in the early going.

"I think that we've got some guys that are obviously shooting much lower percentages than their last season or their career average, almost a handful of guys," he said after Tuesday's game. "And I don't think it's because they're shooting fall-away, totally contested ones. I think they're shots that we want them to take. So I think we're going to continue to take them, and I'm pretty sure those will even out into their career averages."

In particular, Nurse highlighted Dean Wade—a low-volume, above-average three-point shooter—as someone who can help fix the problem.

"We certainly feel like there are shots that he turned down in the first half-dozen games, for sure," Nurse said. "But again, I don't think he's quite in the mindset to take them. We just keep showing him. We need his spacing. It's obviously why we signed him. He's a spacing big, and we need that, for sure."

Ariel Hukporti is out for the year with an Achilles tear. Dominick Barlow has yet to make his season debut because of a back injury. And Adem Bona suffered a cervical spine sprain against the Detroit Pistons and has missed the Sixers' past three games. That's left the Sixers woefully shorthanded in the frontcourt behind Embiid.

Jabari Walker has been filling in as the nominal backup 5, but he's only 6'7″. When Embiid has been off the floor in recent games, opponents have started attacking the basket with aplomb knowing the Sixers don't have a rim protector lurking to swat their shots away.

After the Celtics game, Nurse told reporters that he might have to switch up their defensive strategy whenever they go with a small-ball 5.

"I think we need to just check on it schematically to see if there isn't something we can do different when we're small," he said. "Maybe we can do a little more switching, maybe we can do a little more blitzing, things like that. As you know, we kinda used to use certain coverages, and when Bona would check in, we'd go to some other coverages. We haven't gotten to as much of that this year, but that's probably where we're heading."

On a related note: What's the point in having Tacko Fall signed to a two-way contract if he can't get on the floor even with the frontcourt this depleted? That isn't to suggest that he would have stemmed the bleeding against the Celtics—if anything, they likely would have picked him apart in drop coverage—but the Sixers may need to reevaluate how they're using their two-way spots to add more frontcourt insurance until they get healthier.

The Sixers' schedule considerably softens up over the next few games, which should help deter any Chicken Littles from screaming about how much the sky is falling. But despite their early-season success, the Sixers are still clearly a ways away from being a legitimate title threat this year. The loss to Boston underscored two glaring weaknesses that opponents will happily exploit in the playoffs if the Sixers can't fix them before then.

Unless otherwise noted, all stats via NBA.com, PBPStats, Cleaning the Glass or Basketball Reference. All salary information via Spotrac and salary-cap information via RealGM.

#2845. Cardinals to start rookie QB Carson Beck vs. Rams following Gardner Minshew injury, Jacoby Brissett trade

At 3-6, and with a rash of injuries — including one to their most recent starting quarterback — the Arizona Cardinals are turning to rookie Carson Beck.

Arizona benched and then traded Jacoby Brissett. His replacement, Gardner Minshew, suffered a UCL injury this past week in Seattle during a loss to the Seahawks.

In their absence, the No. 65 overall pick in this year's draft is getting the nod in the desert.

Beck will make his first career NFL start on Sunday when the Los Angeles Rams come to town, head coach Mike LaFleur said Wednesday.

"I know this isn't too big for him," said LaFleur, who voiced that he wants the soon-to-be 25-year-old to let it rip.

The former Georgia and Miami star will be tasked with moving the ball against a defense that features premier edge rusher Myles Garrett and stout defensive tackle Aaron Donald. Beck will have to do so with a thin receiving corps.

Although tight end Trey McBride — who's first among all NFL players this season with 73 receptions — will serve as a nice security blanket for the third-round pick, Beck will be working with a group of wide receivers that's currently led by the likes of Reggie Virgil, Simi Fehoko and Devin Duvernay.

Altogether, that trio has combined for 1,235 career receiving yards, and Duvernay has accounted for 1,025 of those. He's a two-time Pro Bowler but for his return skills.

It's possible Zay Jones is pulled up from the practice squad. Either way, it'll be a lot more difficult for the Cardinals to evaluate what they really have in Beck at this point, especially compared to earlier this season when they had Michael Wilson, Marvin Harrison Jr. and Kendrick Bourne lining up at wide receiver.

Wilson, the team's WR1, is out for the season with a neck injury he sustained in Seattle this past weekend. Arizona dealt Harrison to the Houston Texans last month in Week 5. As for Bourne, he went down with a serious ankle injury during the Cardinals' Week 8 road win over the Dallas Cowboys.

Plus, Jalen Brooks — a 2023 seventh-round pick who impressed in the preseason and recently began making a regular-season impact — is done for the year due to a wrist injury he experienced in a Week 7 loss to the Denver Broncos.

Not only will Beck be playing with a hodgepodge of wideouts, but he'll also be quarterbacking behind an offensive line that's been missing left tackle Paris Johnson Jr. The blind-side protector landed on season-ending injured reserve after he tore his left biceps amid a Week 4 road defeat to the New York Giants.

And, to top it off, a notable draft classmate of Beck's, Jeremiyah Love, is currently on IR. The highly touted running back, this year's No. 3 overall pick, is dealing with his latest ankle issue.

On the bright side, Beck played outstanding in his lone preseason appearance. He got the start in the Hall of Fame Game, throwing for 188 yards and a touchdown on 15-of-19 passing against the Carolina Panthers.

Granted it was the Hall of Fame Game, but Virgil showcased some chemistry with Beck while Fehoko caught Beck's TD pass.

Those former scout team receivers are now part of a first-string offense that Beck will be commanding on Sunday.

"He was mature when he got here," LaFleur said of Beck on Wednesday. "Miami and Georgia, I think he was in two really good environments, two really good cultures to prep him for these kind of moments, whether it be in college and then in the NFL.

"So he's been a pro since he's been here. He puts in the exact amount of work that he needs."

Beck has spent most of the season behind a pair of QBs. Yet Brissett, who started the Cardinals' first eight games, is now on the Baltimore Ravens, and Minshew, Arizona's pick to start last week in Seattle, is on IR.

Perhaps it's fitting that Beck and Las Vegas Raiders rookie quarterback Fernando Mendoza will each get their first career NFL start on Sunday, as they concluded their college careers facing off in the national championship.

"I think you learn through experience, but you also learn through watching other people do it, good and bad," LaFleur said, when asked about first-year signal-callers having time to sit before their number is called.

"So for Carson and having to watch Kirk Cousins over in Vegas with Mendoza, those are beneficial times. But, again, being out there and having to do it is the key."

#2844. CFTC investigates if Kalshi improperly self-certified event contracts

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is investigating whether prediction market platform Kalshi failed to properly inform the agency of proposed event contracts, Kalshi confirmed to CNBC.

Kalshi called it a minor issue in how the contracts were filed and said it is working with the CFTC to address the matter. The CFTC flagged weather-related contracts, Kalshi said. The leading prediction market platform didn't say when the agency began investigating Kalshi. The CFTC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

Politico first reported on the probe on Tuesday, noting charges could happen soon, based on the CFTC's private notification to Kalshi. The platform declined to comment on any possible charges.

Prediction market platforms are allowed to self-certify event contracts without the federal agency's prior approval, as long as they comply with the Commodity Exchange Act. The self-certification allows for faster introduction of new contracts.

"The notice is about a procedural error involving the submission of a handful of contracts, many of which weren't live," said Elisabeth Diana, head of communications at Kalshi. "We are already addressing the issue."

The move is the latest by the CFTC as it seeks to rein in prediction markets, which face growing scrutiny in Washington from regulators and members of Congress.

In August, the CFTC ordered Kalshi to take down sports-related "mention markets" — where speculators can trade on what individuals will say in a broadcast or speech — while it looked into the event contract type.

The agency is also conducting a broad probe into competitor Polymarket, which includes reviewing if the company violated a 2022 settlement with the CFTC to prohibit U.S. traders from accessing its international trading platform. Polymarket's domestic exchange operates separately from the global platform.

Prediction markets are also expected to face heightened scrutiny once the Democratic Congress is sworn into office in January. Rep. Ro Khanna, D-Calif., in a Tuesday social media post on X, called for a ban on sports prediction markets — which account for the overwhelming majority of volume on leading platforms.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2843. Jaguars’ GM James Gladstone Explains View on Trading Away Draft Picks

he Jacksonville Jaguars are 6-2 to start the 2026 NFL season and are going all-in. The team was active at the trade deadline, acquiring several big name players including defensive back Jessie Bates, defensive lineman Davon Godchaux and defensive lineman Jordan Davis. The Jaguars already rank among the AFC's and NFL's top teams, but they have raised their level of play even further and clearly want to reach the Super Bowl.

But acquiring those players came at a cost. The Jaguars sent their 2027 first-round pick, 2027 second-round pick, multiple 2028 late-round picks and a 2029 second-round pick in trades, jeopardizing the team's chances to build a roster of young talent in the future. But for a team well-positioned to make a run this season, general manager James Gladstone made his stance clear in regard to sending away such a big haul to acquire Bates, Godchaux and Davis.

Gladstone, who the Jaguars hired ahead of the 2025 NFL season, previously worked for the Los Angeles Rams under general manager Les Snead. During this time there, the Rams won a Super Bowl utilizing a strategy where they traded several draft picks to acquire high profile stars. Gladstone noted that the team still managed to make a lot of picks, while taking into account the compensatory draft pick process and free agency as a way to build the roster as well.

"It's a very symbiotic process that encompasses a lot of layers to it, but it's certainly not 'F them picks," Gladstone told reporters on Tuesday after the deadline. "While that's been characterized as the approach, it's not necessarily — we actually value picks. We don't always use them on the three days that exist in April."

Gladstone added that while draft picks are important, a part of it involves acquiring proven players over drafting rookies with an uncertain outlook. The Jaguars finished 13-4 and won the AFC South last season, but lost in the AFC Wild Card Playoffs to the Buffalo Bills. The team does not want to take any chances in 2026.

The Jaguars acquired Bates from the Atlanta Falcons in order to bolster the secondary and add a seasoned veteran. Bates, a second round draft pick in 2018, is a three-time All-Pro selection and a 2023 Pro Bowl selection. He is in the midst of a strong season, registering 60 total tackles, three forced fumbles, a fumble recovery and an interception in nine games with the Falcons.

Davis came from the Philadelphia Eagles in exchange for a 2027 first-round pick and a 2027 second-round pick and brings extreme size to the trenches. In nine games for the Eagles, he registered 31 total tackles and 1.5 sacks but it is his run stopping ability that makes him so important for the Jaguars.

Godchaux, a fifth-round pick in 2017, came from the New Orleans Saints and while his 2026 season has not been as dominant as years past, he still brings value. He does not get many sacks, but Jacksonville can rely on him to stop the run as well. Godchaux and Bates will become free agents after the season, while Davis will remain under contract through 2029.

#2840. Mizzou women’s basketball adds pair on signing day

Coach Kellie Harper continues to build her foundation, as two players officially signed with the class of 2027: Athena Thompson and Katie Muller. Here's what there is to know about the two prospects and what they can bring to the team as the Tigers' program continues to rise.

Muller was the first commitment of the class, officially pledging herself to the program in July. Muller is considered a four-star prospect and the No. 89 overall prospect according to ESPN. PrepHoops Iowa considers her the No. 2 prospect in the state of Iowa, playing at Dowling Catholic High School.

She won a state title as both a freshman and sophomore with the Maroons. As a junior, she showcased her outside shooting with a 35% clip on 3-pointers. She chose Mizzou over offers from other Power 4 schools including Ohio State, Kansas and Wisconsin, led by former Mizzou coach Robin Pingeton.

Muller will join her older sister, Ellie, who is currently a freshman on the team. Ellie leads the Tigers with 10 blocks through three games. The two took their initial visit to Mizzou together. While they weren't necessarily a package deal, the two always figured they would end up playing together again.

"When I was committing first, I definitely I talked to her about it and made sure it was somewhere that she would want to go to before I made the final decision," Ellie said.

The Tigers' second and final addition to the class was Athena Thompson, who committed to the program the day before Signing Day. She is originally from Great Britain, but now attends Blair Academy in New Jersey.

She still represents Great Britain on the world stage, playing in the last two U18 Women's Eurobasket Division B. The two seasons before that, she played at the U16 level. Her run in 2026 was cut short with a dislocated shoulder, recording just three points and three rebounds. When she was healthy in 2025, she averaged 18.1 points and four rebounds in 18 games.

Currently, Mizzou is projected to graduate four players following the end of the season. However, three are eligible to return with the new 5-in-5 rule: Abbey Schreacke, Grace Slaughter and Tanyuel Welch.

If they choose not to return, the two incoming freshmen will bring the Tigers up to 10 projected players for the 2027-28 season. This gives coach Harper and her staff a great amount of flexibility in the transfer portal. Here is a look at the potential roster.

Mizzou women's basketball returns to the court against UT-Martin at 6:30 p.m. Thursday at Mizzou Arena.

#2838. Even post-Mitchell Robinson, the Knicks are feasting on the glass

When the Knicks lost Mitchell Robinson in the offseason, there was not only a sense of mourning for losing the services of the longest-tenured Knick right after a championship, but also in the sense of what he provided on the court.

Even with his injury-prone nature, Robinson was a premier rim protector and an anchor for the Knicks' defense whenever he was on the court, which would be enough of an asset on his own, but the biggest asset he provided was something entirely different.

As we all came to know and love, he is simply the best offensive rebounder of this generation and one of the best rebounders, period, in the NBA. His innate ability to gobble up boards helped turbocharge an offense that already had plenty of offensive weapons, but would sometimes struggle with inefficiency.

For several years now, the Knicks have built their offense around the expectation of extra possessions. They've game-planned to win games by simply getting more opportunities to score than their opponents by grabbing more rebounds and committing fewer turnovers. With Robinson gone, the former was in serious danger, even if the team signed Andre Drummond to bandage it.

Through 10 games this season, though, they seem to have not missed a beat.

The Knicks are impressively the No. 1 rebounding team in basketball so far. That's powered by Drummond not missing a beat with his usual rebounding prowess, but also an elevation in the team's process and scheme.

Karl-Anthony Towns, historically a great rebounding center, leads the NBA with a staggering 13.8 rebounds per game. While his defensive strides have been the most encouraging part of the start of this season, he's been a huge part of winning the possession battle by hitting another level with a 36.6 DREB%, which also leads the league.

As a team, the Knicks have hauled in 55.5 percent of available rebounds, 73 percent of defensive rebounds, and 38.8 percent of offensive rebounds, all leading the NBA. They've done this despite routinely having smaller lineups on the court.

Over the last two years, the Knicks were an elite rebounding team, especially thanks to double-big lineups and versatility. They've gradually gotten smaller as a team, have played zero minutes with more than one player taller than 6'8" on the court, and are still dominating on the glass.

Towns, Drummond, and Josh Hart have combined to do a bulk of the rebounding on both ends, but it's also been a group effort. OG Anunoby has been much more active on the glass, averaging 1.7 OREB/g. Mikal Bridges and Jordan Clarkson are grabbing more rebounds on a rate basis. Even Jalen Brunson is averaging a career high in offensive rebounds.

Everyone is feeding off each other. Of the Knicks' 10 most-used lineups, nine of them have an elite rebounding percentage, with the lone exception being a shocker: the starting five (47.5%).

Their early-season struggles, particularly on offense, are worth monitoring, but some positive regression is due whenever Hart returns from his ankle/illness ailments, especially in the rebounding department. The team as a whole played poorly through five games and has only started to pick it up recently.

The synergy has allowed them to just take the soul out of teams in recent games, something that's been pivotal to their success in recent years. Just look at last night's game against Milwaukee, where, despite the plucky Bucks chopping a 26-point lead down to 11, they couldn't overcome a 59-32 rebounding disadvantage.

Against Cleveland on Saturday, the Knicks took advantage of no Jarrett Allen to outrebound the Cavs, 49-40, to help stretch their second-half cushion. In the blowout of the Bulls last week, it was another 57-34 bludgeoning.

It even makes you think: was it the system that made Mitchell Robinson who he was, or was he just a superpowered cog in the wheel?

Let's slow down. Robinson was always a generational rebounder, as evidenced by his work under previous head coaches, but his early struggles in Boston are notable.

As long as Towns continues to be consistent defensively and the team keeps rebounding like this… will they even miss him that much?

#2837. UH in the College Football Playoff? Cougars may not have to wait for the expanded 24-team field

The College Football Playoff expands to 24 teams in 2027.

If you're the University of Houston, why wait?

The Cougars checked in at No. 14 in the latest CFP rankings released Tuesday, a three-spot jump from the previous week.

If the playoff began today, the Cougars would miss the cut in the current 12-team format.

Under the expanded format for next season - which will include the four champions from the Power Four conferences (SEC, Big 12, Big Ten and ACC), the highest-ranked Group of Six champion and 19 at-large selections - the Cougars would be in position to host a first-round playoff game.

How does No. 14 Houston vs. No. 19 Ole Miss at TDECU Stadium sound?

You'll get get in-depth news and analysis about UH's basketball and football teams from reporter Joseph Duarte by signing up for the Cougar Confidential newsletter.

"I wanted to have an opportunity to win a national championship," coach Willie Fritz said this offseason about his decision to take the UH job in 2024.

That path might be arriving ahead of schedule.

To have any chance in this era of college football, you must have an invitation. Otherwise, you can spend your holiday at the Alamo Bowl or Pop-Tarts Bowl. Nothing wrong with those postseason destinations, but the ceiling has been raised for a UH program that is now among the top 15 in the nation.

It's been a hard climb. The last time the Cougars were in the national discussion was during the H-Town Takeover, a two-year window that included wins over behemoths Florida State, Oklahoma and Louisville. But even then, the smallest slip (hello, UConn, Navy and SMU) kept the Cougars from a shot at a national title.

In the decade since, UH has been mostly irrelevant, going through two different coaches and one bad extension before hiring Fritz, who has been a master program builder at every stop and every level. The Cougars went from 4-8 in his first season to 10-3 last year, capped by a win over LSU in the Texas Bowl.

Now, the Cougars enter the final three weeks of the regular season - starting Friday against Colorado in Boulder - still in the hunt for a spot in the Big 12 championship game.

UH will need some help - specifically a loss by Texas Tech - and will need to beat Colorado, West Virginia and Baylor.

Then leave it up to the computers and tiebreaker scenarios.

Under one scenario, UH would win a four-team tie for second place with BYU, Texas Tech and Oklahoma State based on conference opponents' winning percentage.

The winner of the Big 12 championship game receives an automatic bid to the CFP.

In Tuesday's second rankings, Utah (9-0) was No. 2 behind Georgia and in the driver's seat to be the Big 12's highest-ranked team in the playoff. BYU, with a signature win against Notre Dame, dropped only one spot to No. 10 after the Holy War loss to Utah. Texas Tech is No. 12, making the Red Raiders the first team out (the last spot is for the G6 representative). Selection committee chairman Hunter Yurachek said Texas Tech's strength of schedule - ranked 65th by the committee's metric - lags behind that of two-loss teams like No. 10 BYU and No. 11 Kentucky.

Along with Houston, No. 23 Arizona and No. 25 Oklahoma State, the Big 12 can make a strong case as the second-best conference behind the SEC and argue it deserves multiple bids for the first time in the 12-team CFP era.

"I think Houston may need some chaos to happen, but just the fact they're up at No. 14 is pretty crazy," college football analyst Josh Pate said on his podcast.

The Cougars are close to the bubble thanks to a 7-2 start, with the two losses to Utah and Texas Tech by a combined 10 points. What may hurt the Cougars is a remaining schedule that offers no big opportunities to impress the selection committee.

There are bound to be some losses among the top 13, which would open the door for UH. A big game Saturday: Oklahoma State plays Texas Tech in what should be a shootout in Stillwater.

UH currently has a 21% chance to make the playoff, according to ESPN's Playoff Predictor. By winning out, even if the Cougars lose in the Big 12 championship game, their odds would increase to 31%. There could even be a path to the playoff if UH doesn't reach the Big 12 championship game. At 10-2, the Cougars would likely be near the top 10, giving the committee something to consider.

The Cougars understand the stakes are high.

"We can't look forward, we can't look back," quarterback Conner Weigman said. "You've got to be very present in what you're doing and how you handle your business. That's how you win football games this time of year."
