#2836. How Democrats could snarl Trump administration stakes in private sector companies

With Republicans in charge of the White House and Congress, President Donald Trump has embedded the federal government in private sector companies' affairs in unprecedented ways. That could change once Trump's Democratic adversaries take over the House and Senate following the blue wave midterm.

Democrats, planning to root out alleged corruption involving Trump, his family and their business associates, have signaled they will closely scrutinize companies that have curried the president's favor.

Their investigations may extend to Intel and the dozens of other companies that have clinched equity stakes with the government during Trump's second term. Democratic lawmakers in July asked law firm Skadden Arps for details about its involvement in the Intel deal.

The dynamic could affect how Trump approaches relationships with private sector companies, after spending two years involving the government in their affairs through stock agreements or, in the case of U.S. Steel, acquiring a "golden share" when the company was acquired by Nippon Steel.

The golden share, which allows a holder to outvote other shareholders, was negotiated as part of the June 2025 deal. The highly unusual arrangement gives Trump veto power over several key areas of the steelmaker's business, including those related to employee salaries, capital investment and even the company name.

In a unique twist, the company's amended charter specifies that after Trump leaves office, the veto power passes not to his successor, but to the Treasury and Commerce departments as representatives of the U.S. government.

U.S. Steel CEO David Burritt said Wednesday that the government's representative on its board, Commerce Department official David Shapiro, typically abstains from voting but has access to company discussions.

"He gets to hear everything we're doing," Burritt told CNBC's Megan Cassella at the U.S. Chamber of Commerce's B20 summit in Washington, adding that the arrangement has not constrained the company's operations.

"The government has not stepped in and said 'you can't do this or can't do that,' but we know what the rules are," Burritt said.

The U.S. Steel intervention turned out to be just the beginning of the U.S. government taking stakes in companies, a highly unusual move stateside. A month after acquiring the golden share, the Trump administration agreed to a $400 million stock deal with rare earths company MP Materials. The month after that, Trump announced the U.S. would take a 10% stake in chipmaker Intel.

By January, one year into Trump's second term as president, the administration had established some form of equity position in 14 companies, according to the CATO Institute's tracker. That number skyrocketed to 41 by November, including nine such announcements in May alone, the tracker shows. The holdings include both publicly traded and privately held companies, and CATO tracks deals that are closed as well as others that have been made public but are still being negotiated.

Following Republicans' decisive defeat in the Nov. 3 midterm election, the federal government has not revealed any new stock positions. But Trump, entering lame-duck status and on the verge of losing political power over Congress, could keep trying to steer his government to forge more equity deals with companies, said Indiana University associate professor Sarah Bauerle Danzman, who researches the intersection of national security and investment policy.

"He might feel he has less to lose," Bauerle Danzman, who is also a senior fellow at the Atlantic Council, told CNBC.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton told CNBC in a statement, "Through voluntary equity investments in willing companies, the Trump Administration has partnered with the private sector to rebuild America's industrial base and bring critical production back home.""Moving forward, the President is willing to work with anyone to advance solutions that strengthen our economy and put more money back in the pockets of American workers," Middleton said.

But Trump's so-far unimpeded efforts may face sudden resistance once Democrats take majority control of the House and Senate in January.

Democratic lawmakers are openly gearing up to score wins against Trump and his associates as they vow to crack down on alleged rampant government corruption.

Those probes are expected to center on tech giants like Apple, Meta Platforms and Elon Musk's companies, whose executives have spent millions cozying up to the administration. Democrats are also poised to dig into potential conflicts related to the bevy of critical minerals deals Trump has struck. Lawmakers of both parties are also looking at Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, the close Trump ally engulfed in scandal after admitting an extramarital affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

The administration's equity interventions could become a target in that mission, said Bauerle Danzman, predicting that Democrats might seek to boost oversight efforts even if they don't try to cancel the existing stock arrangements.

The next Congress could try to ensure the government is taking stakes that are "forwarding genuine national security priorities" and that do not provide "special favors to political and family connections," she said.

"The story there is more about kind of tying these issues to ones of accountability and anti-corruption, as opposed to necessarily cost," Bauerle Danzman said.

Asked whether U.S. Steel was preparing for potential congressional investigations after Democrats take control, Burritt said he was unaware of any investigation but would cooperate if one materialized.

"I am not aware that they're investigating, but if they are, we're happy to work with them," Burritt said. "It's not a big deal for us because we know we did the right thing."

It's not clear how strongly Republicans would try to defend Trump's golden share or his government's equity stakes should they become a Democratic target.

Conservatives traditionally espouse free markets and denounce excessive government meddling in the private sector. Republicans, including Trump, spent much of the midterm campaign warning that Democrats aim to destroy the U.S. capitalist system and impose economy-killing big government socialism.

Some Republicans have criticized the Trump administration's corporate interventions, but the moves have received little pushback from the president's party overall.

But in the wake of a crushing electoral defeat, some Republicans "might feel emboldened and actually join with the Democrats" in trying to clamp down on Trump, Bauerle Danzman said.

Burritt, despite embracing the government's involvement in U.S. Steel, said corporate leaders should retain responsibility for running their businesses.

"We need to keep accountability at the right place. We need to keep it with, with the leaders of companies and with the companies individually," he said. "We need to be capitalists."

#2835. Meta head of infrastructure will leave and be succeeded by former AWS cloud exec

Meta's long-time infrastructure leader will leave the company in the new year, setting the stage for a recently hired former Amazon Web Services executive to oversee its massive data center technical operations.

Santosh Janardhan, Meta's head of infrastructure and co-head of engineering, revealed his upcoming departure on Wednesday in an internal memo obtained by CNBC. Janardhan, who has worked at Meta since 2009, said "it's time to pass the baton" to former AWS senior vice president David Brown, who joined Meta in July from AWS.

The executive move underscores Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg's efforts to expand the company's data center operations under newer leaders at a time of increasing investor scrutiny on capital expenditure spending by the social media giant and its hyperscaler rivals.

Brown will now formally lead Meta's infrastructure efforts and serve as chief technical officer of the Meta Compute initiative, which is spearheaded by the company's president and vice chairman Dina Powell McCormick and technical leader Daniel Gross. Zuckerberg is relying on these new executives to continue developing cutting-edge data centers and related infrastructure needed to support its AI ambitions.

The company said during its recent third-quarter earnings report that it expects full-year capex to fall in the range of $135 billion to $145 billion. Meta also logged negative free cash flow of $2.12 billion, which S&P Global Ratings noted as part of its decision in late October to revise Meta's credit rating from AA- stable to AA- negative.

Janardhan said in the memo that he will help support Brown through the first quarter of 2027 as part of "the transition."

"As we make the next set of choices about our priorities, this is the right time for Dave to take the lead in shaping that work," Janardhan said in the memo. "Dave has spent his career building and operating dependable infrastructure for a wide range of demanding workloads. He has the instincts, depth and judgment this moment requires."

Bloomberg News first reported on the executive transition.

#2834. Sabres’ Ukko-Pekka Luukkonen Set for Key Test Against Lightning

The Buffalo Sabres are most likely turning to a veteran goaltender in their quest to gain ground in the Atlantic Division.

Per Rachel Lenzi, Ukko-Pekka Luukkonen manned the starter's net on Wednesday during the Sabres' optional morning skate. Daily Faceoff listed him as the "likely starter" in a pivotal Atlantic Division showdown with the Tampa Bay Lightning.

Luukkonen has been struggling overall this season but has looked better as of late. He is 2-1-2 in his last five starts and 5-3-3 overall with an .878 save percentage, a 3.27 GAA, and a .300 quality starts percentage.

He will face a tall task on Wednesday night, should the projection hold, as the Lightning are once again among the league's best teams. Tampa Bay is ninth in goals scored with 63. They also boast one of the best netminders in the world in Andrei Vasilevskiy, who, per Eduardo Encina, is projected to start for Tampa.

Luukkonen will have his work cut out for him. But so will the Sabres' skaters.

On Tuesday and Wednesday, Paul Hamilton shared updates on two players: Ryan McLeod and Louis Crevier. McLeod looks like he is ready to roll, while the jury remains out on Crevier.

McLeod is slated to center the third line, which includes Jason Zucker and Josh Doan at the wings. That entire line is looking to get going, with Doan leading the trio with four points in 17 games.

The top six is projected to include Zach Benson, Noah Ostlund, and Jack Quinn on one line, and Peyton Krebs, Josh Norris, and Tage Thompson on the other. Norris enjoyed a solid outing in that win on Nov. 6 over Columbus.

Beck Malenstyn, Tyson Kozak, and Sam Carrick are the slated fourth-liners. All three players boast an incredibly physical game and should be fun to watch.

If Crevier can't go, Conor Timmins and Zach Metsa should get the nod on the third pairing. Buffalo's projected top four looks consistent, with Mattias Samuelsson and Rasmus Dahlin on one pairing, and Owen Power and Olen Zellweger on the second. Power has 14 points in 17 contests, leading all Sabres defensemen in the category.

Through the first quarter of the season, the Sabres have held their own against the Atlantic. Buffalo is currently 2-1-2 in divisional games and has yet to lose a single game to a rival by more than a goal.

They opened up their Atlantic slate on Oct. 12 in a 5-3 victory over the Florida Panthers. Then came three straight intra-divisional games decided by either overtime or a shootout. Buffalo dropped both matchups to the Montreal Canadiens, who remain perfect in the Atlantic, and defeated the Toronto Maple Leafs.

Buffalo suffered its first regulation loss in the Atlantic against the Ottawa Senators by a score of 2-1. That said, the Lightning present a challenge. Tampa is in third in the Atlantic and poised to tie the second-place Canadiens with a win on Wednesday. Buffalo is trying to keep pace in the early-season wild card race.

Should Luukkonen ultimately man the net on Wednesday night, he'll need to pull off yet another solid outing, similar to what he has had lately. The puck drops at 7:00 p.m. EST.

#2833. Inside India newsletter: Trump's poll setback fuels hope for U.S.-India trade deal by year end

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from India.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

After months of being weighed down by high energy prices, trade uncertainty and foreign investor exodus, India has had plenty to celebrate this Diwali. Foreign investors returned to its market in November and, now, it seems the long-awaited U.S.-India trade deal could be around the corner.

Experts say that the two sides are closer to signing on the dotted line following the U.S. midterm election results than they were before.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

The U.S.-India trade deal, which has been riddled with uncertainty since February, is likely to get a final push to the finish line, thanks to U.S. President Donald Trump's electoral setback in the midterm polls.

Experts told CNBC that Trump could lean toward closing some of the pending trade deals to promote them as policy achievements to the American public, and the pact with New Delhi is a "prime candidate."

They added that Trump and Indian Prime Minister Modi are likely to sign the trade pact when they meet in December for the G20 leaders meeting, as negotiations have largely concluded.

The U.S.-India trade talks "are far along and there seems to be a gentlemen's agreement between Trump and Modi" that the deal should be concluded when the two meet at Mar-a-Lago in December, Mark Linscott, former assistant U.S. trade representative and senior advisor at the U.S.-India Strategic Partnership Forum, told CNBC.

Trump has used tariffs as a pressure tactic to clinch trade deals with several countries, even as that approach has drawn the ire of his political opponents who have flagged that export levies have increased costs for American households. Cost of living was the top concern for American voters in the midterms.

"The election was a verdict on the cost of living, and tariffs are now a liability the President cannot defend on the numbers," Ronak D. Desai, visiting fellow at Hoover Institution of Stanford University, told CNBC.

"Congress will not hand him an affordability win, but a trade deal is one he can announce on his own," Desai said, adding that an announcement of a deal with India is "more likely than it was before the election."

India's commerce and external affairs ministries and the U.S. Trade Representative's Office did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

When asked about how U.S. midterm election results will reshape the ties with New Delhi, a spokesperson for India's Ministry of External Affairs on Tuesday said that the relationship between the two countries "enjoys bipartisan support in the United States."

Trump insists Americans do not share the burden of his tariffs, but according to estimates from Tax Foundation, a nonprofit, the duties raised the average U.S. household's tax burden by $820 in 2026 and $1,000 in 2025.

As a result, the Democrats, within days of taking control of both the houses of Congress are preparing to challenge Trump's signature tariff policy. A trade deal at this time could be seen as a validation of Trump's tariffs-led approach to business.

"The president may lean more into getting deals done – seeing them as policy achievements that he can promote to the American public," Atman Trivedi, partner at Washington-based DGA Albright Stonebridge Group, told CNBC. He added that the Trump administration would like to get a deal done with India by the G20 leaders' summit in December.

While American voters are more affected by import duties on Canadian goods, experts said that a trade deal with India could blunt criticism about tariffs as an effective bargaining chip.

In February, Trump announced a deal with India, claiming that New Delhi had agreed to "Buy American" at a much higher level after the U.S. agreed to slash tariffs to 18%. Trump had earlier imposed a 25% tariff on imports from India, along with an additional 25% duty on goods from New Delhi for buying Russian oil.

But that deal has since been awaiting final approval, especially after the Supreme Court struck down Trump tariffs, giving India space to demand preferential tariffs that give it an edge over competitors.

While American and Indian officials at the start of October said that the negotiations had reached an impasse, Trump on Oct. 20 said that the two sides were "pretty close" to a final deal. As per Indian media reports, the country's Commerce Minister Piyush Goyal confirmed that discussions on the trade deal had largely "concluded."

"We have been hearing for weeks that a deal is close to be settled," Aryan D'Rozario, associate fellow and chair on India and Emerging Asia Economics at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, told CNBC, adding that Modi's visit to the G20 summit in December "will provide the perfect stage to formalize an agreement."

India as an AI hedge? The country's stocks could see robust inflows as markets globally tankThe Indian stock market is emerging as an attractive hedge as investors seek refuge from a brutal sell-off in the artificial intelligence trade, making it a strong contender for global capital. If global investors "neutralize" their underweight positions on India, it could lead to foreign inflows of $115 billion, as per JPMorgan.

India billionaire Adani's firm buys Dutch firm Evos to gain foothold in EuropeAdani Ports and Special Economic Zone, India's largest port operator, will acquire Netherlands-headquartered Evos from Igneo Infrastructure Partners in an all-cash deal at an enterprise value of 2.56 billion euros.

Nov.15: India unemployment rate for October.

#2832. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2831. Bouncing Wynn Shares might be primed for the 'wheel strategy'

In Barry Levinson's classic film Rain Man, Dustin Hoffman's character leans over to his brother in the middle of a blackjack hand with a simple piece of advice: bet two for good, one for bad.

In the case of Wynn Resorts shares, it might be time to bet two for good.

Steve Wynn hasn't exactly been on a winning streak. No longer running his namesake company, he finally sold his 50 Central Park South penthouse after years on the market. WYNN shares haven't really been on a winning streak either. The stock is down more than 27% year-to-date as I write this. It was down more than 46% from the 2025 highs within the past couple of weeks. But it has bounced a bit since then, and perhaps the tables are turning.

Fundamentally, the business is demonstrating exceptional strength, particularly in the United States. Wynn's Las Vegas EBITDA margin has actually surpassed that of its Macau resorts. Recognizing this, management has accelerated capital spending in Las Vegas, where the company's profit has doubled from pre-pandemic levels. Meanwhile, the Asian market is also showing signs of life. Strategic property upgrades in Macau are successfully drawing lucrative premium mass play, which is directly supporting the company's ambitious expansion plans for the Enclave at Wynn Palace.

From a valuation standpoint, WYNN is trading very close to its five-year historical averages across key metrics, including P/E, EV/EBITDA (currently around 9x to 10x), and Price-to-Book Value. Although this represents a slight premium compared to the broader gaming industry mean, Wynn's justifies a premium. (The Encore, the newer of the two adjacent Las Vegas properties, is one of my preferred hotels on "The Strip"). Technically, the stock has admittedly been in a downtrend, but this recent sharp bounce off the late October lows may indicate the run is over. Several technical indicators—specifically the MACD, Fear & Greed Index, Commodity Channel Index (CCI), and several key Moving Averages—are now aligned, suggesting it may be time to go long.

With the next earnings report not scheduled until February, the stock has a clear runway free of binary fundamental catalysts, yet implied volatility remains surprisingly high. This environment makes buying options expensive and short premium strategies more attractive.

Rather than betting on the roulette wheel, traders should look once again to "the wheel" strategy. Something I discussed earlier this week on United Rentals. The strategy? Step one, sell cash-covered puts below the current market price to collect that inflated premium. If WYNN stays above your strike price through expiration, you keep the cash and repeat the process. If the stock dips and you are assigned, you acquire shares at a discount. Once assigned, you move to step two: selling covered calls against your new stock position to generate continuous income until the shares are eventually called away, after which you revert back to step one and play again. A methodical, high-probability way to get long a world-class operator while getting paid to wait.

#2830. Nets Two-Way F Nelson Sets Career High in G League Win

Brooklyn Nets two-way forward Grant Nelson had a career night for their G League affiliate, Long Island, in its 142-106 win over the College Park Skyhawks on Tuesday.

Nelson scored 18 points on 8-for-14 shooting and grabbed a career-high 17 rebounds. He and Dain Dainja combined for 30 of the Nets' 59 rebounds compared to the Skyhawks' 37.

The 6-foot-11 forward dominated in the short roll with Ben Saraf. College Park committed two defenders to a driving Saraf, leaving Nelson wide open for rim-rocking finishes.

His mindset to finish with authority should be a fresh sight for Brooklyn. Day'Ron Sharpe and Moritz Wagner have a softer touch around the rim to compensate for their lack of lift when there's no open runway.

It potentially gives the Nets a lob threat. They've missed one since trading Nic Claxton to the Chicago Bulls as part of the four-team Julius Randle trade.

His chemistry with Saraf down in the G League suggests he could have the same success with rookie Mikel Brown Jr. and Nolan Traoré. The young point guards play with pace, coming off the screen and probing for created advantages for themselves or teammates.

Brooklyn would have to call him up from the G League, which is unlikely with Sharpe and Wagner healthy. If an injury forces either of them to miss time, or one rests on a back-to-back, Nelson could find his opportunity with the parent team.

Nelson impressed in the preseason during his brief stints. He averaged 6.8 points in eight minutes per game, including a preseason best of nine points and four rebounds against the Washington Wizards on Oct. 12.

The 24-year-old also showed rim protection on Tuesday with two blocks. He displayed discipline with his verticality and timing his jump to block shots.

Brooklyn lacks a true rim protector. Sharpe and Wagner don't fit that mold, and Wagner admitted he prefers to guard the perimeter.

Nelson fits that role with three blocks in two games. He's dominating the G League scene and building a case for the Nets to call him up at some point this season.

The two-way forward scored 18 points and grabbed 13 rebounds in Long Island's season-opening loss to the Greensboro Swarm last Wednesday.

Brooklyn's youth is impressing in their G League assignments. Saraf recorded a 21-point, 12-assist double-double last Saturday, and rookie Joshua Jefferson opened the season with 15 points.

#2829. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2828. Social Security could pay full benefits until 2059 if taxable payroll kept pace with GDP: AARP report

Social Security's taxable payroll, which provides most of the revenue for the program that pays monthly benefits to millions of Americans, has fallen as a share of gross domestic product over the past four decades, according to a new report from the AARP Public Policy Institute.

Had payroll tax revenue kept up with GDP, the program could pay full benefits until 2059, according to the research — an additional 25 years beyond the latest projected depletion date of 2034 for the program's combined funds. The AARP Public Policy Institute provides policy research, analysis and development at AARP, a nonprofit, nonpartisan organization that represents Americans ages 50 and over.

"Social Security's finances have weakened not simply because America has grown older, but because the economy has changed, while the program's revenue system has not," Richard Johnson, vice president of financial security at the AARP Public Policy Institute, said during a Tuesday press briefing on the research.

"America's economy is larger and more productive than it was four decades ago, but Social Security is receiving support from a smaller share of that economy," Johnson said.

Social Security's trustees projected in June that the program's combined retirement and disability trust funds may be depleted in the third quarter of 2034. Unless Congress takes action, 83% of scheduled benefits will be payable at that time, according to the trustees' projections.

The trustees' report also included an earlier projected depletion date for Social Security's retirement trust fund — the fourth quarter of 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable. However, the AARP said it based its analysis on the later 2034 date because Congress has shifted money between the funds in the past.

The program's current shortfall is worse than lawmakers had anticipated in 1983, when the last major Social Security reforms were enacted.

Rising income inequality means the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings — a percentage lawmakers expected to remain constant when they made the 1983 reforms, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized there, according to the institute.

AARP points to an aging population as another reason for Social Security's funding challenges. The worker-to-beneficiary ratio will fall from 3.4 to 2.4 between 2000 and 2030. Falling fertility rates are also shrinking the worker-to-beneficiary ratio.

Shifting income patterns have also meant that a smaller share of U.S. GDP is subject to the Social Security payroll tax. Taxable payroll has fallen from 41% of GDP in 1983 to 34% as of 2025, a level Social Security's trustees project will remain, according to the AARP.

AARP attributes the decline to a shift from wages toward business and investment income, which generally isn't subject to payroll taxes. Automation has reduced demand for workers, and pass-through businesses allow some owners to report compensation as profits rather than wages, according to the report.

Social Security would have collected $10.8 trillion in additional funding between 1984 and 2034 if taxable payroll had stayed at 41% of GDP, according to the AARP. That figure is in inflation-adjusted 2025 dollars.

In 2026, up to $184,500 in wages are subject to the Social Security payroll tax.

Payroll taxes accounted for 91% of Social Security's revenues in 2025, according to AARP's research, while 5% came from interest income and 4% was through federal income taxes paid by high-income beneficiaries on their benefits.

To increase Social Security's revenues, lawmakers may consider changes that make high-income taxpayers contribute more to the program while sparing low- to moderate-income taxpayers, according to the AARP report.

"The fix for Social Security isn't cutting earned benefits; it's rebuilding the revenue behind them," Johnson said.

Raising the payroll tax threshold — which is incrementally adjusted each year to keep up with average wages — would not affect most workers, since just 6% of individuals had earnings at or above the cap in 2024, according to the AARP.

In 2027, the Social Security taxable maximum is set to increase to $192,300.

Bills have been introduced in Congress to raise the cap to apply to higher thresholds, such as $250,000 or $400,000, or to eliminate it altogether. Getting rid of the cap would close 67% of the 75-year funding gap if workers do not receive a benefit increase in exchange for those contributions, according to AARP research based on the Social Security Administration's assessments.

Another option: Lawmakers may also shore up the program by taxing income not currently subject to Social Security payroll taxes, according to the AARP, following an increase in business activity through pass-through entities — such as sole proprietorships, partnerships, limited liability companies and S corporations.

Lawmakers could create a new tax on investment income to help fund Social Security's trust funds. A new 12.4% tax on investment income for active S corp. officers and limited partners would close about 25% of Social Security's 75-year financing gap, according to the AARP, which cited research from Social Security's actuaries.

One option not mentioned in the AARP research is raising the payroll tax rate, which is currently 6.2% for workers and employers, or 12.4% combined. The Social Security trustees estimated that an increase in the combined rate to 16.65% beginning in January 2026 would have made the combined program solvent for the 75-year period ending in 2100.

A national survey conducted in February found 62% of individuals would support gradually increasing the payroll tax from 6.2% to 6.5%, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. That poll included 2,509 adults.

The 1983 Social Security reforms included changes to benefits, such as gradually raising the retirement age from 65 to 67, delaying the cost-of-living adjustment by six months and taxing Social Security benefits.

Research shows those changes have reduced lifetime retirement benefits by about 19% for people turning 65 in 2026 compared to what they would have received under the previous rules, Jenn Jones, vice president of financial security and livable communities at the AARP, said during the Tuesday press briefing.

While the AARP has not endorsed any specific benefit changes, Jones said the organization opposes cuts to monthly checks.

"Millions of Americans — we hear from them every day — cannot afford to lose a penny," Jones said.

#2827. Rams Named Potential Suitor for $108 Million Wide Receiver

The Los Angeles Rams and head coach Sean McVay entered the 2026 NFL season as Super Bowl favorites to many. What has transpired since has been a nightmare.

No, the Rams are not out of playoff contention. In fact, they currently hold a 5-4 record entering Week 10 action against the Arizona Cardinals.

What has caused the nightmare if they are still very much alive as a contender in the NFC? Injuries have completely derailed the season that they were supposed to have.

Myles Garrett is back, but he has missed most of the season due to a knee issue. Davante Adams is currently out with a ruptured hamstring. Most recently, Puka Nacua went down and is expected to miss extended time due to a knee injury suffered on the final play of last week's win.

At wide receiver, things have gotten bad. It may not help this season, but Los Angeles has been linked as a potential suitor for a $108 million wideout during the offseason.

Oct 12, 2026; Inglewood, California, USA; Los Angeles Rams head coach Sean McVay reacts during the fourth quarter against the Buffalo Bills at SoFi Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Jayne Kamin-Oncea-Imagn Images

Bleacher Report has released a new column looking ahead at the top free agents of the upcoming offseason. When it came to Parker Washington, they listed the Rams as one of the top potential landing spots for the Jacksonville Jaguars' impending free agent.

Not only did they named Los Angeles as a team to watch, they also projected that Washington will receive a four-year, $108 million contract in free agency.

"While a lack of elite size may keep Washington out of the upper tier of receiver contracts, the 24-year-old should command a deal that falls somewhere between those signed by Wan'Dale Robinson and Alec Pierce this past offseason," they wrote.

Washington would be a huge addition for the Rams. With Davante Adams set to become a free agent as well, he would make perfect sense as a long-term replacement.

Back in 2025, Washington had his breakout season with 58 receptions for 847 yards and five touchdowns. In eight games this year, he has racked up 29 receptions for 493 yards and five scores.

Washington is just 24 years old right now. He's going to be one of the most sought after playmakers in free agency. Watch out for Los Angeles as a potential suitor.

#2826. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2825. Prediction markets tied to clinical trials and drug approvals draw lawmakers' ire

Prediction market event contracts on both clinical drug trials and Food and Drug Administration regulatory approvals are drawing attention from two Democratic senators, according to a letter obtained first by CNBC.

Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, D-Md., and Sen. Ron Wyden, D-Ore., expressed concern over the related event contracts in a Wednesday letter to Department of Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. The senators asked what measures HHS has taken to mitigate any risks that may arise from the markets.

"Allowing individuals to bet on the success of these trials and drugs undermines the scientific process, creates perverse incentives for researchers, harms trial participants, and could lead to dangerous medical outcomes for untold numbers of Americans," the senators wrote.

Alsobrooks and Wyden repeated concerns raised by some healthcare professionals that the markets could alter patient thinking when considering whether to participate in a trial and expressed concern over potential insider trading risks from researchers and regulators who may have access to nonpublic information.

The senators requested HHS provide all internal communication regarding staff participation in prediction markets tied to matters governed by the department. They also requested all communication between the department and the prediction market platforms themselves, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — which regulates event contracts — and any internal analyses that have been done regarding the potential impact of the markets.

"HHS, FDA, and [the National Institutes of Health] must uphold the integrity of the drug development process and maintain public trust in the public health by putting essential guardrails in place to prevent individuals profiting on the success and approval of drugs that the American people depend on," Alsobrooks and Wyden said in the letter.

Both leading prediction market platforms, Kalshi and Polymarket, offer the contracts, although in Polymarket's case the markets can only be found on its international exchange, which isn't regulated in the U.S.

When Kalshi launched its related markets in July, the company said it put in place the guardrails, such as only allowing markets related to late-stage trials, in order to limit the risk of trading. Kalshi also vowed to list contracts only after a trial had completed enrollment and to use employee verification technology for all traders participating in the markets.

"Drug development is one of the most important and most information-constrained industries on earth," Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said in July. "The data that determines which drugs advance and which don't is largely locked away from the people who need it most. Surfacing information is what Kalshi is for, and we are committed to doing it right."

Kalshi noted that investors also buy shares in companies in fields including biotechnology and pharmaceuticals that are awaiting regulatory approvals.

"The hypothetical concerns of the Senators apply equally to the equities market, which appears to be left out of their letter.," Kalshi spokesperson Jack Such told CNBC. "The only difference is that the size — and incentive for bad activity — is significantly higher in the equities market than it is on Kalshi."

Polymarket and the Department of Health and Human Services didn't immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2824. CoreWeave soars more than 24% for best day since April 2025 after strong Q3 report

CoreWeave shares jumped 24.71% Wednesday for its best day since April 2025 after the neocloud posted quarterly earnings that beat estimates and raised its guidance for the year.

"Demand for the infrastructure is and remains overwhelming." CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Wednesday.

Neoclouds such as CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN have come under scrutiny this month as investors assessed the sustainability of the AI trade. CoreWeave, in particular, sparked alarm when spreads on its CDS widened past 11%, generally viewed as a distress level, as investors have to pay up for protection against default. With the price of its high-yield debt climbing, CoreWeave highlighted additional routes to finance its data center build-out with less alarming yields, quelling investor concerns for the time being.

"Our asset level debt has basically been rock solid through this entire thing," Intrator told CNBC. "There is an enormous demand for that product. We're about to launch another asset-level debt in the next week or two that has already been rated high investment grade." Intrator added that he expects it "will be underwritten in the market at 7% to 7.5% interest," which will bring down the average weighted cost of capital for the company.

The three months to Sept. 30 marked a "breakout quarter" for CoreWeave, Intrator said on the conference call, adding that growth was supported by multiple sources of funding, including financing backed by customer contracts and around $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits.

Revenue jumped 168% to $3.7 billion in the third quarter, the company said in a Tuesday statement.

Other neclouds also rose on CoreWeave's strong results, with Nebius rising 15.44%, Applied Digital 7.37% and Iren 5.63% Wednesday.

Despite fears that a "blue wave" in last week's midterm elections could restrict data center capacity, Intrator reiterated that data center growth looks poised to continue as expected for CoreWeave.

"We don't see any impact on our ability to deliver the data centers within the U.S. that are required for us to deliver on our backlog," Intrator told CNBC on Wednesday. "In Texas, Governor-elect [Gina Hinojosa], said that facilities that are permitted and under construction will continue, and we've been really, really thoughtful about the broad distribution."

CoreWeave expects $1.275 billion to $1.375 billion in adjusted operating income on $13 billion to $13.3 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by Visible Alpha were looking for $1.07 billion in adjusted operating income, and analysts polled by LSEG had expected $12.86 billion in 2026 revenue.

The neocloud has done well to allay concerns about financing by moving away from unsecured bond financing, Zacks Chief Equity Strategist John Blank told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"It might be the bottom for CoreWeave," Blank said Wednesday. "But the shares are so beaten up, you have to believe at this point there's more value on the upside than the down."

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Caleigh Keating contributed to this report.

#2822. LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome

LeBron Log No. 4: A courtside sip, 16,000 makes and Cleveland's welcome originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James' latest week with the Sixers included a sip of a stranger's wine, yet another number no NBA player had reached and a return to the city where his career began.

It also included his worst performance with Philadelphia, followed less than 48 hours later by his highest-scoring game with the team.

Welcome to the fourth edition of the LeBron Log.

No chronicle of James' first season in Philadelphia could omit the time he helped himself to a fan's wine during a game.

James chased Tyrese Maxey's transition pass into the stands during the third quarter of the Sixers' Nov. 2 win over the Bulls. He collided with Philadelphia native Troy Clemons, spilled some of Clemons' wine and sampled what had landed on his arm.

The interaction received its own story on our site, and deservedly so. It also belongs in the Log.

Clemons did not keep the cup as a souvenir. He threw it away and got another drink.

James returned to the court and later broke into a dance after scoring over the 6-foot-1 Tre Jones. The wine clip generated millions of views across social media and became the defining image from an otherwise ordinary 19-point Sixers victory.

The following night in Detroit, James offered a broader explanation for why Philadelphia has already looked comfortable together.

"We've got a good team," James said. "Guys vibe with one another. Guys like each other. Guys enjoy being around each other. That has a lot to do with it, too. It makes the game a lot easier when you enjoy being around people."

Eleven games into his Sixers career, James appears to be doing exactly that.

James arrived in Detroit two baskets away from another unprecedented number.

His second field goal against the Pistons made him the first player in NBA history to reach 16,000 regular-season field goals.

James finished the Sixers' 112-106 victory with 13 points, 10 rebounds and eight assists. The near-triple-double helped Philadelphia improve to 8-1 and avenge its only loss of the season.

Kareem Abdul-Jabbar ranks second in league history with 15,837 made field goals. Karl Malone is a distant third at 13,528. James is the only active player with more than 12,000.

His scoring record has long been secure. James passed Abdul-Jabbar in career points in February 2023 and later overtook him in made baskets. The round number in Detroit still illustrated the distance he has created between himself and every other scorer.

James began his career by making 5,361 field goals across seven seasons with Cleveland. He added 3,510 in Miami, returned to make 3,691 more for the Cavaliers and made another 3,421 during eight years with the Lakers.

Basket No. 16,000 belonged to Philadelphia.

The Sixers' 8-1 start also carried a notable James connection. Each of his previous four teams to begin a season at least 7-1 reached the NBA Finals.

James was not ready to entertain that conversation in early November.

"It's too early to be talking about the Finals and things of that nature," he said. "We've got to continue to buy into what we want to do."

Two nights later, James returned to the place where nearly half of those 16,000 baskets were made.

His first game in Cleveland as a Sixer brought out jerseys from every stop of his career. Cavaliers colors filled Rocket Arena alongside Heat black, Lakers purple and the Sixers' throwback look.

James received a warm ovation when he was introduced last among Philadelphia's starters. When he checked out late in the fourth quarter, with the result decided, the crowd stood for him again.

James has experienced both ends of Cleveland's emotional range.

He returned as a member of the Heat on Dec. 2, 2010, to one of the most hostile receptions in NBA history. Six years later, he delivered the Cavaliers their first championship and ended Cleveland's 52-year major professional sports title drought.

"I've seen the depths of hell when it comes to a reception here, all the way to the heavens above," James said. "Anything in between is easy money."

The reception fell somewhere in the middle. His performance landed much closer to the bottom.

James finished with eight points on 3-for-12 shooting, three assists and six turnovers in the Sixers' 119-95 loss. It was his first single-digit performance in Cleveland since he scored three points against the Rockets on Dec. 30, 2004.

He had scored at least 10 points in 396 consecutive regular-season games in the city. The run covered his first stint with the Cavaliers, every visit as an opponent and his entire second tenure with the organization.

It ended during his first trip back in a Sixers uniform.

"I definitely did not feel in a good rhythm offensively," James said. "A couple of them were careless. A couple of them were just trying to make the right play and not being successful with it."

It was only the 10th single-digit regular-season game of James' 1,630-game career. He still took time afterward to consider what an eventual jersey retirement in Cleveland could represent.

"The journey from being an 18-year-old rookie, to leading a franchise to a championship," James said. "And everything in between."

James did not carry the rough night into Chicago.

Less than 48 hours after struggling in Cleveland, he scored a season-high 22 points in the Sixers' 127-121 victory over the Bulls. Thirteen came during the second quarter, when James helped Philadelphia turn an early deficit into a halftime lead.

His first basket produced another milestone.

James entered Sunday trailing Abdul-Jabbar's 1,895 points for the most scored against the Bulls in NBA history. His first-quarter three-pointer moved him into the lead. He ended the afternoon with 1,916.

The most interesting play from his night was a pass.

With the Sixers ahead by four and 2:20 remaining, James drove into the paint. Three Chicago defenders collapsed toward him. Maxey stood between the left wing and corner, but even he did not believe James could see him.

James fired the ball through the defense. Maxey sidestepped Isaac Okoro and made the three-pointer, pushing Philadelphia's lead to seven.

After his postgame interview, James pulled out his phone and watched the play again.

"He didn't think I saw him," James called out from his locker as reporters asked Maxey about the possession.

Maxey acknowledged that he had positioned himself to rebound because he expected James to shoot.

"Honestly, I was like this," Maxey said, raising his hands. "I thought he was about to shoot a layup."

James has spent the opening weeks of the season teaching his new teammates to expect the unexpected pass.

"You've got to become a wide receiver or a catcher at that point," Maxey said. "You never know when you're going to get the ball.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2821. Fantasy Football Rankings: Hayden Winks' RB Blueprint for Week 10

The Blueprint is my weekly column and basically everything that I use to build out the most accurate rankings I can. There will be this RB column, a WR column and a combination column on the onesie positions. You will find film notes and endless charts. Maybe enough charts to stress test the Yahoo website. I kid.

It's impossible to get every ranking thoroughly early in the week, so be sure to keep checking in on these columns through Sunday morning. As always, my full rankings at every position are available.

1. Jahmyr Gibbs (vs. NE) - 134.5 proj. total yards

I'd keep starting Gibbs, personally. He's half PPR's all-time RB26 through the first eight games.

This week, the Lions are back to a 27.0-point team total (fourth) as 2.5-point home favorites against the Patriots, who struggled against THE Kaleb Johnson last week. New England is typically better, however, allowing just 17.1 half PPR points to RBs this year (28th). But it also hasn't faced Gibbs!

2. Kenneth Walker III (at ATL) - 111.5 proj. total yards

After a couple of mediocre games against top-end defenses, Walker predictably rebounded against the Jets. He's back to the RB2 on RB2 usage this season. In fact, Walker is half PPR's all-time RB34 through the first eight games.

The Falcons have been lit up against RBs recently with some injuries at EDGE and LB, allowing Bucky Irving (170 total yards), Christian McCaffrey (RB6) and D'Andre Swift (RB5) to run wild. They also just traded away S Jesse Bates this week. This is a potential big game with the Chiefs projected for 24.5 points as 3.5-point road favorites.

3. Christian McCaffrey (at DAL) - 98.5 proj. total yards

Since cramping in Australia, CMC is the RB3 on RB4 usage. Good reminder to touch grass instead of panicking based on a small sample.

This is an eruption spot against the Cowboys, of course. The 49ers have a 29.5-point team total in a game projected for 55.5 total points. Dallas has allowed the fourth-most fantasy points to RBs this year, even allowing Tyler Allgeier to drop 191 total yards in Week 8. I expect Kyle Shanahan to toy with these Dallas LBs and Ss all game.

4. Bijan Robinson (vs. KC) - 118.5 proj. total yards

Bijan rebounded in Week 9 after the offense went away from him as a receiver in the month prior. His six receptions were really refreshing to see. He still handled 19 carries, too, with Brian Robinson Jr. on injured reserve. The Falcons traded away their starting C at the deadline as a sign of what this season is for Atlanta, but it's still a well-coached OL. Robinson is still in line to finish as a top-5 RB, as he's started the year as the RB4 on RB10 usage, even if it's been more volatile than we had hoped.

Kansas City is 28th in half PPR points allowed to RBs, while both the Falcons (44%, 30th) and Chiefs opponents (46%, 30th) sit in the bottom three in neutral pass rate. Atlanta enters with a 21.0-point implied team total (18th) as 3.5-point home underdogs. It's not a clean matchup for Robinson, but the game plan will be to let him attempt to take over.

5. James Cook III (at NYJ) - 103.5 proj. total yards

Cook has scored 16.7+ half PPR points in seven straight games. He's up to the RB5 on RB7 usage across this season, almost identical to how he placed last year. He's averaging 102 rushing YPG (RB3) on the second-most carries. Managing 107 rushing yards against the Vikings last week is the most recent feather in his cap.

This matchup is far, far easier. It's an eruption spot against the Jets, who are missing star DT T'Vondre Sweat (IR). They've allowed the seventh-most rushing YPG (128) and have drastic on/off splits with Sweat. Kenneth Walker III just finished as the RB1 overall against them last week, and Cook was the RB2 overall in Week 2 of last year against them. The Bills have the highest team total of the week (29.5).

6. Chuba Hubbard (at NO) - 103.5 proj. total yards

There have been some OL changes, and it'll be nice when star LG Damien Lewis returns, but Hubbard's role is extremely stable each week. He's cleared 12.7 expected half PPR points in 8-of-9 recent games, leading Hubbard to an RB6 on RB14 usage ranking. If you look up mid-range RB1 in the dictionary, Hubbard's face appears.

This is an eruption spot. The Saints have allowed the most schedule-adjusted fantasy points to RBs, allowing the RB room to add 26 more yards than usual. They've allowed 134 rushing YPG (fourth), despite Chase Young and Carl Granderson providing pass-rush juice at EDGE. It won't help that they traded away a DT this week. The Panthers project for 23.0 points (12th).

7. Javonte Williams (vs. SF) - 83.5 proj. total yards

Williams is continuing his RB8 season and will now have another backup behind him in Kendre Miller following Tyler Goodson's injury. Volume and goal-line chances are never a concern here, as he's the RB9 in carries, RB21 in receptions and the RB1 in inside-the-5-yard-line carries. He has three more carries at the goal line than anyone else in the league.

This week is no different. The Cowboys project for 26 points against the 49ers, and that game total of 55.5 points is one of the highest of the entire season. San Francisco lost one of its up-and-coming DTs recently, but Nick Bosa is back and DeForest Buckner was recently added. This is a better DL now than it was last month. The Niners already sit as the second-best defense in rushing YPG (80.9) this year, too. The good news is the Cowboys' OL had their best rushing performance of the year last week, leading to over 3.0 yards before contact. It's one of the best lines in the NFL quite easily, especially in run blocking.

8. Bhayshul Tuten (at TEN) - 83.5 proj. total yards

Tuten had his lowest usage of the year in Week 9 because the Jaguars rolled the Ravens and Tuten was legitimately questionable with an ankle injury. He's had extra time to heal and should be back to his usual fringe-RB1 self now. Tuten was the RB13 on RB18 usage removing last week, despite only being the RB39 in receptions per game. He's a big play and goal-line threat in a potent offense. It appears that Liam Coen trusts him a whole lot more now than before.

The Titans are a joke. They don't have superstar DT Jeffery Simmons and also traded away S Amani Hooker. They're on a 3-game streak of allowing the starting RB (Chase Brown, Quinshon Judkins and Jonathan Taylor) to clear 101+ total yards. Jacksonville hits the road as 10.5-point favorites with an implied team total of 28.5 points (third).

9. Derrick Henry (vs. LAC) - 87.5 proj. total yards

It's best to just throw out last week with Joe Fagnano. Henry has played in seven games without Lamar Jackson and Fagnano since the beginning of last year, and Henry is the RB5 on RB3 usage in them. That's the optimistic case with Jacoby Brissett coming in to settle things down. He'll be loaded up with carries as long as the game script allows for it.

The winless Chargers provide just that. The Ravens are 4.5-point home favorites with an implied team total of 21.5 points (17th). Los Angeles has allowed 122 rushing YPG this year and just traded away LB1 Daiyan Henley and placed S1 Derwin James on IR. Henry's outlook is significantly better this week than last, especially with star first-round LG Vega Ioane and C Cam Jurgens in the lineup.

10. Kyren Williams (at ARI) - 89.5 proj. total yards

Williams is running poorly recently. Blake Corum is playing every other drive. That has been true for a very long time. The real drivers over this RB24 on RB23 usage month have been 1) bad TD luck and 2) the Rams not being as good as expected. Williams only has two touchdowns this month, while Corum has four. The Rams being unable to stretch the ball vertically in the pass game has led to fewer scoring drives in general (15th in scoring and 17th in EPA/play this month).

The latter is scarier for his fantasy RB1 status, as Matthew Stafford looks tiers worse on tape individually this year and now won't have either top WR for the next few weeks. Williams could see his receiving volume climb to counteract the loss of projected points, but his lower TD equity is impacting him.

The good news is the Cardinals are up next. Los Angeles has a 24.5-point implied team total (ninth) against one of the worst offenses I've seen on paper. Williams has finished as the RB24, RB20, RB9, RB9, RB21, RB1 and RB2 against Arizona throughout his career.

11. Jaylen Warren (at CIN) - 92.5 proj. total yards

Since Rico Dowdle returned, Warren is the RB10 on RB14 usage, seeing 58% of the carries. He's been the RB18 or better in usage every week since Week 3, while being one of the most efficient players. Only Kenneth Walker III is ahead of him in rushing yards over expected (+26%), and this Steelers OL deserves a lot of credit with C Zach Frazier playing like the best in the league. The amount of explosives he's generated is unsustainable, but Warren easily looks like an upside RB2 at the very least.

The Bengals have superstar DT Dexter Lawrence to account for, but their EDGE, LB and S groups have struggled to contain RBs. Bijan Robinson and Tony Pollard have posted RB3 overall finishes in back-to-back weeks against them. As the lead back in Week 3, Warren was the RB8 on 176 scoreless yards. (That was the sniper game that took away a touchdown from him, if you remember that.) Pittsburgh heads on the road as 3.5-point road underdogs with an implied total of 23 points (14th).

12. Jonathan Taylor (vs. MIA) - 85.5 proj. total yards

The trade deadline was not kind to Taylor. The Colts kept him, possibly with the idea being that they can trade him in the offseason and save some face as the rest of this season winds down. But they also traded away superstar LG Quenton Nelson, star DT DeForest Buckner, WR Josh Downs and WR Keenan Allen. This team is simply way worse now. This is how Taylor has finished, depending on how well his team has been over a huge sample:

The good news is the Dolphins are just as bad, especially with Malik Willis headed to IR now. Indianapolis enters as 3.5-point home favorites with an implied total of 21 points (19th).

The thing to watch for here is if Taylor is a 70% or 90% player with Seth McGowan flashing some potential as his backup. Last week's goal-line touchdown was due to Taylor unfortunately losing his shoe, but McGowan has mixed in before garbage time over the past month. That wasn't the case at the peak of Taylor's fantasy value. Taylor is the RB19 on RB13 usage this month.

13. Chase Brown (vs. PIT) - 71.5 proj. total yards

Brown had his second-lowest usage (9.0 expected half PPR points) of the season in last week's blowout win against the Falcons. This month, Brown is the RB39 on RB16 usage, handling RB35 carries and RB3 receptions per game. The lack of rushing production has really hurt him, particularly near the goal line. Here's what happened down there last week:

Brown is having a bad season and is holding onto RB2 value in the most optimistic angle solely because the Bengals are averaging 30.0 points (second).

Brown flipping the switch against a loaded Steelers defense would be a surprise, as they've allowed the third-fewest rushing YPG this year. They have at least allowed the fourth-most fantasy points to RBs via the receiving game (8.1 half PPR points). Offenses facing Pittsburgh have passed the ball in neutral situations at the highest rate (60%). Cincy is already leading in that metric (66%), so Brown saving face with 4-6 receptions wouldn't be surprising. The Bengals are 3.5-point home favorites with an implied total of 26.5 points (fifth).

14. Blake Corum (at ARI) - 66.5 proj. total yards

Corum is on fire. He's the RB14 on RB37 usage this month. The Rams have the RB1 and RB2 overall in rushing success rate right now, after also finishing there last season. They'll be RB-reliant here with both Puka Nacua and Davante Adams looking weeks away. Corum will continue to rotate drives with Kyren Williams, and whoever is on the field for their scoring drives will be the one to finish with the better outing. Corum's floor is about 12 carries without a target, so he's as TD-reliant as they get.

The good news is the Cardinals are bad. Los Angeles has a 24.5-point team total (ninth), and the Cardinals have allowed 122 rushing YPG this year. Corum was the RB14 with 60 yards and a score against them in Week 6 and was the RB26 and RB3, respectively, against them last season. To my surprise, Arizona didn't trade away any of its defensive starters at the deadline.

15. Woody Marks (at CLE) - 78.5 proj. total yards

In three games without David Montgomery, Marks hasn't broken out despite an increased workload. He's not very good, nor is the Texans OL, but he is still a positive regression candidate, assuming he'll continue seeing 20.0 touches per game, leading to RB18 usage.

The Browns are a fine defense, checking in the middle of the pack in most metrics. They've recently fallen off, allowing Audric Estimé (RB9), Jaylen Warren (RB10) and Tony Pollard (RB1) to finish as fantasy RB1s. Those aren't loaded offenses, either. Houston travels as 4.5-point road favorites with an implied team total of 22 points (15th).

I'm falling for it again. Please save me.

16. Cam Skattebo (vs. WAS) - 61.5 proj. total yards

#2820. Mississippi Senate: Scott Colom lead over Hyde-Smith pops to more than 4,400 votes

Democratic nominee Scott Colom on Wednesday significantly expanded his lead over incumbent Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith in the Mississippi Senate election to more than 4,400 votes as the ballot count continued.

When the day began, Colom had a 2,990-vote lead over Hyde-Smith.

That spread ticked upward slowly until just after 2 p.m. ET when a surge of newly counted ballots boosted his tally by 607 votes, and then soon after by another 621 votes.

As of 2:30 p.m., Colom was ahead of Hyde-Smith by 4,404 votes, according to the Associated Press.

With an estimated 99% of the votes in, Colom led Hyde-Smith by 49.18% to 48.72%. Independent candidate Ty Pinkins had 2.1% of the vote.

Colom has steadily expanded his lead over Hyde-Smith for days as he has won a higher share of so-called affidavit ballots than he received in ballots counted on Election Day night. Affidavit ballots are cast by voters on a provisional basis if there is a question about the voter's eligibility.

The AP estimates that fewer than 15,000 ballots are left to be processed.

Colom was boosted by newly counted votes from Hinds County, the second-most populous county in Mississippi, which encompasses the capital of Jackson.

Most of the county's residents are, like Colom, Black.

If Colom is elected, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the Reconstruction Era after the Civil War.

In 2024, Democratic presidential nominee Kamala Harris defeated President Donald Trump by 46 percentage points in Hinds County. As of Wednesday morning, Colom was winning 80% of the vote in the county, with just 19% for Hyde-Smith.

Mississippi, which has not elected a Democratic senator since the segregationist John Stennis in 1982, was on the radar of few, if any, political analysts as a possible pickup for Democrats.

Tuesday was the deadline for resolving affidavit ballots. It is also the deadline for the receipt of mail-in absentee ballots that are postmarked by Election Day, Nov. 3.

Mississippi is one of just two states whose Senate election has not been called. Alaska is the other.

Democrats so far have flipped seven Senate seats held by Republicans going into the election. That gives Democrats a new majority in the Senate of at least 54 seats in the 100-member chamber come January.

Alaska before Election Day was viewed as possibly key to the question of whether Republicans would retain their narrow majority in the Senate.

In Alaska, Democratic nominee Mary Peltola's lead extended to 4,250 votes over incumbent Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan, with 84% of the total votes counted, a margin of 1.8 percentage points.

If neither she nor Sullivan gets more than 50% of the total vote, the race will go to a ranked-choice round, in which the two other candidates, Gerald Heikes and another candidate named Dan Sullivan, will be eliminated, and the second choices of voters who made them their first choice will be allocated between Peltola and the incumbent.

#2819. Steve Holland blamed for Man United’s alarming fall-off in training standards this season

Manchester United won their only Premier League match this season back in August, and the club are currently languishing in 18th place in the league table.

This has left head coach Michael Carrick on the brink of receiving his marching orders, and there are valid reasons for INEOS to pull the plug.

Leaving aside the club's transfer failings in the summer, this group of players should be doing far better than their current form suggests.

One of the biggest complaints this season has been the Red Devils' lack of intensity off the ball, with the team recording woeful running statistics throughout the campaign.

They have reportedly been outworked in almost every game, with the i Paper attributing this to Steve Holland's reduced role in training.

Last season, the former Chelsea assistant manager and the rest of Carrick's backroom staff (Jonathan Woodgate, Jonny Evans and Travis Binnion) all garnered praise for the way they brought the squad together after the turbulent Ruben Amorim era.

This term, things have unravelled quickly on the field, but it has not been made clear why Holland no longer holds the same sway over the players.

"Carrick's backroom staff drew much praise last season for how well they complemented each other. Steve Holland brought some much-needed experience to the training pitch, while Jonathan Woodgate, Jonny Evans and Travis Binnion all bonded well with the players.

"However, several sources indicated they noticed a decline in intensity during training. One insider insisted Holland has taken a more back-seat role in recent months, which has contributed to the drop in training standards."

Holland is known to be a hard taskmaster, but even he has failed to light the fire in this group of players as Carrick reportedly prepares to leave.

Now, the onus could reportedly fall on Mauricio Pochettino, who has earned a reputation for making his teams run harder than any other side.

Whether he can translate the same intensity at Old Trafford remains to be seen.
